## 🎬 Lecture 019 — Losing International Traffic: How Hreflang Tags Solve Region Confusion

### 🎭 The problem story (the Shopify dropshipper)
You launch a **dropshipping business on Shopify** selling **AI-generated motivational posters and t-shirts** for productivity enthusiasts. It's going well, so you expand to **US, UK, France, India, Japan and Thailand**, creating separate store versions with **local currencies**, **region-specific pricing based on purchasing power parity**, and **translated motivational phrases** for non-English markets.

A month later your analytics show something alarming:
- **UK customers see prices in US dollars.**
- **Japanese visitors see English text instead of Japanese.**
- **Thai customers bounce immediately** — they can't understand the content.
- **Almost everyone globally is shown the US version.**
- Search engines are **confused by apparent duplicate content** across country versions.
- Customers abandon purchases when they see foreign currencies; **conversion rates plummet in every market except the US.**

**What went wrong? You forgot to implement hreflang tags.**

### 🌍 What hreflang is
**Hreflang tags are HTML elements, just like canonical tags.** You use them to **tell Google which language and country a specific page targets.** Use them whether you have **nearly identical content with small changes (different prices/images)** or **pages in entirely different languages** — they **avoid duplicate-content problems** (that's why they're "similar to canonical tags"). Their main job: **make sure the right page reaches the right audience in a specific country speaking a specific language.**

> A basic hreflang tag says *"this page is for English speakers in the United States,"* but typically you'll have **4–5 lines, each targeting a specific country/language combination.**

### 📏 Coding conventions (follow precisely)
1. **Language codes:** two-letter **ISO 639-1** codes, **lowercase** — e.g. **`en`** (English), **`ja`** (Japanese).
2. **Country codes:** two-letter **ISO 3166-1 Alpha-2** codes, **uppercase** — e.g. **`US`** (United States), **`TH`** (Thailand).

**Common examples:**
| Value | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `hreflang="en"` | English content targeting **all regions** |
| `hreflang="en-US"` | English content specifically for the **US** |
| `hreflang="fr-FR"` | French content specifically for **France** |
| `hreflang="x-default"` | The **default page to show when no specific language/region match exists** |

**Four strict formatting rules:**
1. **Language code always lowercase** (`en`, not `EN`).
2. **Country code always uppercase.**
3. **Only a hyphen as separator** — `en-US`, **not** `en_US` or `en US`.
4. **Language first, then country.**

### 🛠️ Three ways to implement (good to know all of them)

**Method 1 — HTML `<head>` implementation (sites with fewer than ~100 pages).**
Add the hreflang tags in the `<head>` of each HTML page. (If you think "do I need this on an English-only site?" — **if you target multiple countries or languages, even with English content, hreflang is essential.**)
```html
<link rel="alternate" hreflang="en-us" href="https://example.com/us/">
<link rel="alternate" hreflang="en-in" href="https://example.com/in/">
<link rel="alternate" hreflang="x-default" href="https://example.com/">
```
*(Illustrative form; the lecture shows its own example on screen.)*
- **`rel="alternate"`** → this link points to an **alternative version** of the current page.
- **`hreflang`** → the **language and region** that version targets (e.g. `en-in` = English speakers in India).
- **`href`** → the **URL** of that specific version.
- **`x-default`** → which version to show when the user's language/region **doesn't match** any of your versions.
- ⚠️ **Every page must include the complete set of hreflang tags pointing to all alternate versions, including itself.** If your US page points to your UK page and all others, then **the UK page must also point back to the US page and all others** (reciprocal).

**Method 2 — XML sitemap implementation (very large sites: thousands or tens of thousands of pages).**
Add all hreflang versions **below each URL in the sitemap**. Difference: instead of the HTML `link` tag you use **`xhtml:link`** because a sitemap is XML, not HTML. **Advantage:** manage hreflang information **in one sitemap file (or a few) rather than updating hundreds of individual HTML pages.**

**Method 3 — HTTP headers (non-HTML content).**
For **multilingual direct image URLs, PDFs and other non-HTML content**. The example shows the **same motivational image translated into different languages** — e.g. product images with text overlays in different languages — so users speaking different languages get the right version. ⚠️ This **requires server-side configuration → typically needs developer help.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 020 — Is Your Hreflang Implementation Actually Working? Here's How to Check

### 🛠️ Check with Screaming Frog
1. Open **Screaming Frog SEO Spider**.
2. **Configuration → Spider → Crawl** → make sure **"Crawl hreflang"** is checked.
3. Click **Start** to crawl.
4. **Configuration → Crawl Analysis** → make sure **hreflang** is selected in the list of analyses.
5. When analysis completes, go to the **hreflang tab under Overview** and review.

### ❌ 11 common hreflang mistakes (made by new SEO experts)
1. **Missing hreflang** — pages that should have tags but don't (implemented on some pages, forgotten on others).
2. **Missing return links** — page A links to page B, but B doesn't link back to A. *Example:* the US page has all three versions but the UK page has only two. **Fix:** add the **complete set of hreflang tags to every page version.**
3. **Inconsistent language/region in return links** — reciprocal links use different language codes. *Example:* the US page refers to the UK as **`en-GB`**, but the UK page refers to the US as just **`en`** (not `en-US`) → confuses search engines. *Real story:* an e-commerce site's **French page (`fr-FR`) referred to the US page as `en` rather than `en-US`** → French users searching on **google.fr** were sometimes shown the **US version with dollar prices instead of the French version with euros.** **Double-check language codes match across all reciprocal sets.**
4. **Non-canonical return links** — an hreflang tag points to a URL that doesn't match the page's actual canonical URL. **Hreflang URLs must exactly match canonical URLs — including HTTPS, subdomains (if any), trailing slashes and even case sensitivity.**
5. **Noindex return links** — hreflang points to pages **blocked from indexing**. Contradiction: *"it's like saying 'here's my other email address, but do not save it.'"* **Fix:** remove the `noindex` meta robots tag **or** don't include that page in your hreflang set.
6. **Incorrect language/region codes** — invalid codes or formatting errors are "extremely common". **Always use standard ISO codes with proper formatting**; check the official ISO language and country code lists if unsure (a link is in the lecture resources).
7. **Multiple entries** — duplicate hreflang declarations for the same language/region, often because tags were added in **multiple places or by different plugins.**
8. **Missing self-reference** — like self-referencing canonicals, **every page should include a hreflang tag pointing to itself.**
9. **Missing `x-default`** — you haven't specified which version to show when the country/language doesn't match.
10. **Placed outside the `<head>` section** — hreflang must be inside the head, **not the body** (same as canonical tags).
*(The lecture says "11 common mistakes" but names the ones above; "self-reference vs. canonical" is also noted — the hreflang self-reference should not simply reuse the canonical tag.)*

> 🏁 **Practical challenge (5 minutes):** crawl your international website, check the **hreflang tab for errors**, identify the most common issues. **If you don't use hreflang but need it,** write a simple implementation plan: **which pages need hreflang tags, which language/region codes you'll use, and which implementation method fits (HTML or XML sitemap).**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 022 — How Structured Data Transforms Your Search Results & Boosts Clicks

### 🛋️ The client story (Wayfair)
In a weekly virtual meeting a client asked why **CTR was dropping with no change in positions.** Arun used the **Ubersuggest Chrome extension**, which shows **approximate traffic each search result gets** for a search term. The client was puzzled: *"How can **Wayfair, ranking #5**, get more traffic than **New York Magazine and Amazon, ranking #3 and #4**?"*
**Why:** #3 and #4 show **a plain blue link, a basic description and just sofa images in the thumbnail (not sofa beds)**, whereas **Wayfair's result shows star ratings, free delivery and return information neatly in the SERP** — and a **sofa-bed thumbnail**. The reason: **properly optimized structured data.** The client immediately asked how to implement it.

### 🧩 What is structured data?
- You (a human) see the fully rendered page and know it contains product prices, review ratings, recipe cooking times. **But Googlebot only sees the HTML code**, and crawling/indexing is resource-intensive — search engines **can't read and understand your content word by word.** They need help understanding special information on a page → **structured data**.
- It acts as **a translator between your website and search engines**, using the vocabulary **schema.org**, created by **Google, Bing, Yahoo and Yandex** to be **a universal standard** — *a special language all search engines understand.* *(The lecture calls it "a JavaScript code"; in practice the preferred format is JSON-LD, shown in the next lecture.)*
- Adding it tells Google: *"this number isn't just any number — it's a product price"*; *"this isn't just text — it's a customer review with 4.8 stars."*
- Search engines can then create **rich snippets** (enhanced results with star ratings, prices and other visuals).

### 📈 The result
Your listing changes from a basic blue link to an **eye-catching rich snippet** that **captures more screen space**, **displays valuable info (prices, ratings, availability)**, and **attracts more clicks even from lower positions** — boosting **CTR by up to 30%**.
- *Client case:* an e-commerce client added **Product schema** → **CTR jumped from 2.3% to 3.1% in just two weeks with no change in ranking positions.** The difference came entirely from the enhanced listings.

### 🏷️ The most-used (high-impact) schema types
| Schema type | What it shows in search |
|---|---|
| **Product Schema** | **Price, availability, ratings** for e-commerce items |
| **Recipe Schema** | **Cooking time, ingredients, calories, ratings** |
| **Local Business Schema** | **Address, phone number, business hours** |
| **Article Schema** | **Author, published date, featured image** |
| **FAQ Schema** | **Expandable questions & answers** directly in search results |
| **Review Schema** | **Star ratings and review counts** |
| **Event Schema** | **Dates, locations and ticket availability** |

---

## 🎬 Lecture 023 — Implementing Schema: The Step-by-Step Process Anyone Can Follow

*(Walkthrough with a real example from a **coffee shop website**.)*

### The format
**Google recommends JSON-LD** for structured data — code you add to your page's **`<head>` section** (the lecture describes it as "a JavaScript code"). On screen it shows what it looks like **for a product**. It looks complicated for non-developers — "but don't worry, I'll show you how to generate it without writing it yourself."

### 🔍 Step 1 — Identify schema opportunities (Screaming Frog)
1. Run a **Screaming Frog crawl + analysis**.
2. **Overview → Structured Data tab → look at the entries under "Missing".**
3. **Prioritize:** **high-traffic pages** that could benefit from rich snippets; **product, local business and FAQ pages first**; and **pages in positions 3–10** — *rich snippets can significantly increase CTR for these results.*
4. Also check **Validation Errors** and **Validation Warnings** for multiple URLs. **If you get those warnings, you haven't enabled structured data while crawling:** go to **Configuration → Spider → Extraction** and **enable all options under Structured Data**, then run crawl and analysis again.

> 🔑 Structured data does more than boost SERP CTR: if you want to rank for **voice search** or in **AI search (Perplexity, ChatGPT Deep Research, Grok, DeepSeek DeepSearch)** you need optimal implementation. Arun typically sees **a 15–30% increase in organic traffic within 2–3 months, even without ranking improvements.**

### ✍️ Step 2 — Generate the code (3 options)
**Option A — AI (what Arun uses; saves hours).** Prompt Gemini:
> *"Generate JSON-LD structured data for a coffee product with these details: …"* (then give the details).
⚠️ **Copy/attach/paste the documentation for that schema type** into the prompt — *AIs/LLMs may not be up to date on this and can make mistakes.* Gemini produces the exact code and sometimes suggests improvements. ChatGPT, Grok or DeepSeek also work.

**Option B — a technical SEO schema markup generator** with a simple form: **select the schema type → fill in the fields → copy the generated code.**

**Option C — WordPress: Yoast SEO.** Enable its **structured data modules**, fill in simple forms per page, and the plugin **generates and inserts the code automatically.**

### 📌 Step 3 — Add it to the page
**Copy the generated JSON-LD and paste it into the `<head>` section of your page.**

### ✅ Step 4 — TEST it (where most SEOs go wrong)
The common thinking: *"Not many SEOs implement structured data… I've done so much, 200 more pages to go… AI won't make a mistake in this simple code, testing isn't needed."* **Wrong. "Never trust that your code works without testing it."**
- Use **Google's Rich Results Test**: enter your **page URL** or **paste your JSON-LD code** → click **Test URL / Test code** → review the results and fix errors.
- **If you get errors:** screenshot the error, paste it into Gemini/ChatGPT and ask it to resolve it.
- **Many URLs to validate?** Use **Screaming Frog.**

🔗 **Resource:** Google Rich Results Test → https://search.google.com/test/rich-results

---

## 🎬 Lecture 024 — 4 Schema Mistakes That Can Get Your Site Penalized

*"Didn't I just tell you to never skip testing? Because most probably you'll make one of these four mistakes."*

### ❌ Mistake 1 — Using a schema type that doesn't match the content on the page
Adding schema for content elements **that don't actually exist on the page.** *Real audit:* a site added **Recipe schema to a cooking blog post that contained no actual recipe (no ingredients or instructions)** — and the owner approached Arun **because Google had penalized them.** ✅ **Correct:** use **Article schema** for that blog post.

### ❌ Mistake 2 — Missing essential (required) properties
**Each schema type has required properties; Google will completely ignore your schema if any are missing.**
- **Product schema** must include **at least one of: `review`, `aggregateRating`, or `offers`.** The bad example misses all three → Google ignores the schema.
- Although only one is required, **Google may show a warning if you provide only `offers` without `review` or `aggregateRating`** — so **for optimal rich results, include more than the minimum.**
- Check each type's requirements in **Google's structured data search gallery.**
- 💡 **Arun's rule: "If you're going to implement schema, go all in."** His full Product schema includes **multiple product images, detailed specifications, accurate pricing with return policy, verified reviews with pros and cons, and even related accessory information** — "search engines reward this level of detail with enhanced visibility and specialized features like product knowledge panels."

### ❌ Mistake 3 — Schema data that doesn't match what users see (fake/inflated data)
(Different from Mistake 1.) The page shows **4.2 stars from 178 ratings**, but you think *"how would Google check?"* and enter **5 stars with 203 ratings** in the schema to look better and boost CTR. ⚠️ **Google strictly enforces that schema data must match what users actually see — else you might be penalized.** Arun has seen sites **lose all rich snippets because they included fake reviews in schema.**

### ❌ Mistake 4 — Syntax errors
**Even minor syntax errors break the entire implementation** — e.g. **missing commas or brackets actually matter.** Your code must be syntax-error-free. That's why **Google's Rich Results Test is essential even when you think it looks correct.**

> 🏁 **Practical task:** pick **one important page**, identify **which schema type applies**, use **ChatGPT or a schema generator** to create the code, **implement it, and test with Google's Rich Results Test.**

---

## ✅ Section 14 Recap

- **Is it worth it?** Yes — high pay ($70–120K+ US; ₹6–18L India; $100–200/hr freelance), job security vs. AI, essential for large/multilingual/e-commerce/JS-framework sites; small sites get ~80% from basics; service owners should know enough to hire/evaluate.
- **Technical SEO** = make it easy to **discover, crawl, index and understand** — code, server, structure, files.
- **XML sitemap:** `<loc>`, `<lastmod>`, `<priority>`; Yoast/Shopify/Wix auto; Screaming Frog (500 URL free) / XML-Sitemaps.com / AI for small sites; exclude admin/search/cart/checkout/robots-blocked/tag & author archives; **<50 MB & <50,000 URLs** (use index files), **absolute canonical URLs**; submit in GSC → Sitemaps.
- **Robots.txt:** gatekeeper for bots only; `User-agent`, `Disallow`, `Allow`, `Sitemap`; specific rules beat general; case-sensitive; one Disallow per line; **doesn't deindex** (use `noindex`); don't block JS files; check in GSC → Settings → robots.txt report + Screaming Frog "Blocked by Robots.txt".
- **Canonicals:** tell Google the master URL; self-referencing + cross-domain; avoid chains, multiples, relative URLs, `noindex` + canonical, tags outside `<head>`, unlinked targets; audit via Screaming Frog; GSC "Duplicate without user-selected canonical"; `inurl:` for parameter duplicates.
- **Internal links:** funnel informational → commercial → transactional; fix **orphans, broken links, redirect chains**; contextual, descriptive anchors; breadcrumbs; link from high-authority pages; update old posts.
- **URLs:** keywords (not forced), short (<60 chars ideal), hyphens, lowercase, hierarchical, no parameters/session IDs, **301 redirect** when changed (no chains).
- **JavaScript SEO:** Googlebot crawl → render (~**5 s** limit) → index; CSR vs SSR vs hybrid; Walmart +40%; problems: slow APIs, big bundles, lazy loading, blocked JS; test with View Source vs Inspect, GSC URL Inspection, Screaming Frog JS rendering; fixes: SSR (Next.js/Nuxt), prerender.io, WP Rocket, noscript.
- **Hreflang:** `en` lowercase + `US` uppercase, hyphen, `x-default`; HTML head / XML sitemap (`xhtml:link`) / HTTP headers; reciprocal & self-referencing; 10–11 common mistakes.
- **Structured data:** JSON-LD + schema.org → rich snippets (+CTR up to 30%; 2.3%→3.1% example); types: Product, Recipe, Local Business, Article, FAQ, Review, Event; generate with AI/generator/Yoast; **always test (Rich Results Test)**; avoid wrong type, missing required properties, fake data, syntax errors.

**🔗 Section resources:** Section Notes → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/notes-fix-technical-seo-problems · Hub (tools, prompts, checklists) → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/fix-technical-seo-problems-google-hates · Screaming Frog → https://www.screamingfrog.co.uk/seo-spider/ · XML-Sitemaps.com → https://www.xml-sitemaps.com/ · Google Search Console → https://search.google.com/search-console/ · WP Rocket → https://wp-rocket.me/ · Prerender.io → https://prerender.io/ · Rich Results Test → https://search.google.com/test/rich-results


> 🧐 **How to read the 🔄 blocks:** everything above this line is the course *as taught* (kept unchanged). The block below is an independent *objector* review (researched 2026-10-24) saying where the old way is outdated, wrong or beatable, and what a senior practitioner would do instead. Items marked *unverified* rest on secondary sources. A second pass re-checked the highest-impact claims against primary sources; its corrections are shown in the 🔬 box at the top of each affected block, and the full evidence is in the final appendix.

## 🔄 Objector's Review — Better / Updated Way (Section 14)

> 🔬 **Second-pass verification (primary-source check, 2026-10-24) — corrections to the review below:**
> - **"5-second render limit":** Google's JavaScript SEO docs document **no** 5-second limit (they only say queueing "can take longer" than a few seconds). Google does **not explicitly deny** it either — say *"not a documented Google rule"*, not *"refuted by Google"*.
> - **Confirmed against Google pages:** sitemap `priority`/`changefreq` ignored (only accurate `lastmod` matters); canonical is a strong *signal*, not a directive; hreflang values are case-insensitive; dynamic rendering is described as a workaround; Google gives no title-length rule and may rewrite titles.
> - **Spam-policy wording:** the page heading is *"Site reputation policy"* (not "abuse"); scaled content abuse and expired-domain abuse are listed; generative AI appears only as an example under scaled content abuse.

Review date: 2026-10-24. Scope: README-v2.md Section 14 only. Primary sources = Google Search Central / OpenAI / Screaming Frog pages fetched during this review; secondary = news/blogs (flagged). Items I could not source are marked "unverified".

---

### 1. Sitemap fields: `<priority>` and `<changefreq>`
**Lecture ref:** 004 (XML sitemaps; recap line) | **Old way:** Sitemap has loc/lastmod/priority; "enable lastmod, priority and change frequency" in Screaming Frog and XML-Sitemaps.com; priority 1.0 vs 0.8 "nudges crawlers"; short changefreq "affects crawl budget".
**Verdict:** ❌ Wrong (priority/changefreq); ⚠️ incomplete (lastmod).
**Better way:** Google states it "ignores `<priority>` and `<changefreq>` values". Only `<loc>` plus an accurate `<lastmod>` matter. Google uses lastmod only if it is "consistently and verifiably" accurate and reflects the last significant update (main content, structured data, links), not a footer/copyright change. Senior steps: (1) drop priority/changefreq from generators; (2) have the CMS/build emit lastmod from real content-change timestamps (not build time, not "now" on every request); (3) audit that lastmod values are not all identical or all today (a common cause of Google distrusting the field); (4) segment sitemaps by template/type (products, articles, categories) and compare submitted vs indexed per sitemap in GSC Page indexing to find the indexing problems by template. Google's crawl-budget doc also recommends lastmod for updated content.
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog 24.x sitemap config (untick priority/changefreq); advertools (Python) `sitemap_to_df` to bulk-audit lastmod distribution (tool known to me; not verified in this review); claude-seo `seo-sitemap` skill; GSC Sitemaps report.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/sitemaps/build-sitemap (fetched 2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/large-site-managing-crawl-budget (fetched 2026-10-24)

### 2. Sitemap limits, image sitemaps, submission
**Lecture ref:** 004 | **Old way:** <50 MB and <50,000 URLs; include images; submit in GSC; add to robots.txt; "AI-generated sitemap.xml" for small sites.
**Verdict:** ✅ Limits and submission still correct. ⚠️ Two caveats.
**Better way:** Limits confirmed (50 MB uncompressed / 50,000 URLs). Submission methods confirmed: GSC report, Search Console API, `Sitemap:` line in robots.txt. Caveats: (a) The "sitemap = how fast Google indexes you / fixes the construction-company client in a couple of days" framing is over-promised; a sitemap is a discovery hint, not a guarantee of indexing, and Google only crawls what it deems worthwhile (see crawl-budget doc: relevant mainly for 1M+ page or 10k+ fast-changing sites). Set client expectations accordingly. (b) A hand/LLM-written sitemap is brittle: generate programmatically from the source of truth (CMS/DB/framework route list, e.g. Next.js `sitemap.ts`), validate with a script, and never let an LLM "invent" URLs. Sitemap "ping" endpoint: the build-sitemap page no longer mentions it; treat pinging as retired (my recollection of Google's 2023 retirement; unverified in this review).
**New tools/skills:** Framework-native sitemap generation; Python/advertools validation; GSC API; claude-seo `seo-sitemap`.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/sitemaps/build-sitemap (2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/large-site-managing-crawl-budget (2026-10-24)

### 3. IndexNow / faster discovery (missing topic)
**Lecture ref:** 004 (sitemaps "get you indexed faster") | **Old way:** Only sitemaps; no mention of push protocols.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete.
**Better way:** IndexNow (Bing, Yandex, Naver, Seznam, Yep) lets you push changed URLs instantly. Google does not support it as of 2026 per secondary sources (no primary Google statement found: unverified). For Google keep sitemaps + accurate lastmod + internal links + GSC URL Inspection "Request indexing" for a handful of URLs. The Indexing API remains officially limited to job postings and livestream content; don't use it for general pages. Worth adding IndexNow if Bing/Copilot/ChatGPT-adjacent visibility matters (Bing data feeds several AI products: this link is common industry belief, unverified here).
**New tools/skills:** IndexNow plugins (Yoast/Rank Math/Shopify native since May 2025 per secondary source), Cloudflare Crawler Hints; claude-seo `seo-technical` covers IndexNow checks.
**Sources:** https://searchengineland.com/indexnow-now-officially-co-sharing-urls-between-microsoft-bing-and-yandex-378562 (secondary, 2021 launch); 2026 adoption claims from secondary blogs only (unverified)

### 4. Robots.txt: syntax and rule precedence
**Lecture ref:** 006 | **Old way:** "Specific rules override general ones"; one Disallow per line; case-sensitive; Allow/Disallow/Sitemap/User-agent.
**Verdict:** ✅ Mostly correct, ⚠️ missing details.
**Better way:** Confirmed: Google supports only user-agent, allow, disallow, sitemap; `crawl-delay` is not supported by Google. Precedence = most specific rule by path length; on conflict "least restrictive" wins. Field names are case-insensitive; path values are case-sensitive. Add: wildcards `*` and `$` supported; Google reads only the first 500 KiB; robots.txt cached up to 24 h (so a bad deploy can persist for a day: add robots.txt to deploy monitoring). The lecture's "Disallow /search + Allow /search/about" examples are right under the path-length rule but explain the real rule (length, not "specific vs general" intuition) so equal-length conflicts are resolved correctly.
**New tools/skills:** GSC Settings > robots.txt report (lecture correct that it replaced the old tester); Python `urllib.robotparser` is NOT Google-compliant for precedence: use Google's open-source robots.txt parser (google/robotstxt, known to me; unverified in this review) for testing; Screaming Frog custom robots.txt testing; drift monitoring (claude-seo `seo-drift`).
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/robots/robots_txt (fetched 2026-10-24)

### 5. Robots.txt and AI crawlers (missing; lecture mentions "AI chatbots" and Amazon blocking GPTBot only in passing)
**Lecture ref:** 002, 006 | **Old way:** robots.txt is about Googlebot; AI bots mentioned only as an aside.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete / outdated for 2026.
**Better way:** Treat AI bots as separate user-agent decisions per purpose, not one switch:
- Google-Extended: standalone product token controlling whether Google-crawled content is used for Gemini training and grounding; it "does not impact a site's inclusion in Google Search" (so blocking it never removes you from Search or, per Google's AI-features page, is not needed to appear in AI Overviews; AI Overviews/AI Mode are controlled by Googlebot access plus nosnippet/data-nosnippet/max-snippet/noindex).
- OpenAI: GPTBot = training; OAI-SearchBot = ChatGPT search surfacing (block it and you disappear from ChatGPT search answers); ChatGPT-User = user-initiated fetches, robots.txt "may not apply". Changes take about 24 h to propagate.
- PerplexityBot / Perplexity-User: Cloudflare (Aug 2025) alleged Perplexity used undeclared stealth crawlers to evade robots.txt; Perplexity disputes this. So robots.txt alone is advisory for some bots; enforce with WAF/bot-management and verified IP ranges.
- Decision framework: a business that wants AI citations should ALLOW OAI-SearchBot and search/grounding bots, and may DISALLOW training-only tokens (GPTBot, Google-Extended, CCBot) depending on policy. Log-file analysis (server/CDN logs) is the only reliable way to see who actually crawls.
- Google states no special schema, llms.txt or "AI text files" are needed for AI features (so do not sell llms.txt as a Google requirement).
**New tools/skills:** Cloudflare AI Crawl Control / bot management; log analysis with Python or Screaming Frog Log File Analyser; claude-seo `seo-geo` (AI crawler accessibility audit).
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/crawling/docs/crawlers-fetchers/google-common-crawlers (2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/appearance/ai-features (2026-10-24); https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/bots (2026-10-24); https://the-decoder.com/cloudflare-accuses-perplexity-of-secretly-crawling-sites-despite-explicit-blocks/ (secondary, Aug 2025)

### 6. Robots.txt vs noindex vs deindexing
**Lecture ref:** 006, 009 | **Old way:** Robots.txt can't deindex; use noindex.
**Verdict:** ✅ Correct, with an important operational trap to add.
**Better way:** If a URL is disallowed, Googlebot cannot see its noindex tag, so a "Disallow + noindex" combination keeps URLs indexed (URL-only). Order of operations to deindex: allow crawling, serve noindex (or 404/410), wait for recrawl, only then (optionally) disallow. Also Google's crawl-budget doc: noindex does NOT save crawl budget (Google still requests then drops the page); robots.txt does not reallocate budget to other pages; permanently removed pages should return 404/410 (a "strong signal not to crawl again") and soft 404s waste budget. Lecture 012's "nofollow on internal links to hide pages" is weak: nofollow is a hint and not a reliable crawl control; use noindex/robots/auth as appropriate.
**New tools/skills:** GSC URL Inspection (shows "Indexing allowed? / blocked by robots.txt"), Screaming Frog Indexability column.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/large-site-managing-crawl-budget (2026-10-24)

### 7. Canonical: "tells Google" vs strength of signal; 301 vs canonical
**Lecture ref:** 008, 009 | **Old way:** Canonical = "master copy", "index this one and ignore the duplicates"; if URLs don't match, "Google prioritizes the canonical URL".
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Overstated.
**Better way:** Google documents redirects as a strong signal, rel=canonical as a strong signal, and sitemap inclusion as a weak signal; none are directives and Google may pick a different canonical (GSC "Google-selected canonical" differs from "User-declared canonical" in URL Inspection). The lecture never states this and never covers: (a) when to use a 301 rather than a canonical (if the duplicate need not be accessible to users, redirect; canonical is for URLs that must remain reachable, e.g. filtered/sorted views, syndicated copies); (b) that canonical works only if the pages are near-duplicates (otherwise ignored); (c) canonical does not consolidate crawl load: Google still crawls duplicates. For faceted/parameter URLs at scale, combine canonical with internal-link discipline (don't link to parameter URLs), consistent sitemap URLs, and robots.txt rules only for pure crawl-trap parameters (and never rely on robots.txt for canonicalization: Google says "Don't use the robots.txt file for canonicalization").
**New tools/skills:** GSC URL Inspection (user vs Google-selected canonical), URL Inspection API in bulk via Python/MCP; Screaming Frog Canonicals + Crawl Analysis.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24)

### 8. Canonical + noindex "never combine"
**Lecture ref:** 009 | **Old way:** "Google stops reading the page before reading the canonical... Do not combine them."
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Right conclusion, wrong mechanism.
**Better way:** Google's docs say not to use noindex to pick a canonical within a site ("We don't recommend using noindex to prevent selection of a canonical page within a single site"); use rel=canonical. Mueller (2018) said the two are contradictory signals and Google picks one (generally the canonical), and that noindex might be applied to the canonical target (2012 quote, secondary). Google does read both tags; it is a conflict, not a parsing cut-off. Also Google's Dec 2025 JS doc update advises against generating noindex with JavaScript, and says canonicals are best set in HTML (JS-injected possible but "we don't recommend"; only one canonical on the page). Fix wording: "contradictory signals; pick one."
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog "Canonicalised + Noindex" filters; GSC "Alternate page with proper canonical tag" / "Excluded by noindex" reports.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/javascript/javascript-seo-basics (2026-10-24); https://www.searchenginejournal.com/google-dont-mix-noindex-relcanonical/262607/ (secondary, 2018); https://ppc.land/google-clarifies-javascript-rendering-for-error-pages-in-december-documentation-update/ (secondary, Dec 2025)

### 9. Canonical advice: "cross-domain canonical on scraped content gets copied, boosting you"; "Unlinked canonical targets ignored"; self-referencing everywhere
**Lecture ref:** 009 | **Old way:** Scrapers copy your canonical and give you a boost; unlinked canonicals are ignored; add self-referencing canonicals to every page.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Partly speculative.
**Better way:** Self-referencing canonical is good practice (kept). The "scraper copies canonical = boost" idea is a folk claim with no Google source found; treat as unverified and don't rely on it (use DMCA, monitoring, and strong first-publication signals). "Unlinked targets ignored" is not stated in Google's docs I fetched (unverified); the documented advice is to use consistent, strong signals (internal links, sitemap, redirects) pointing to the preferred URL. The advice "you don't need to find duplicates" holds only for templated duplicates (parameter variants rendering the same template with a self-canonical); for multiple distinct URL paths you still need redirects/canonicals configured server-side.
**New tools/skills:** Copyscape/Google search snippets for scrape monitoring; GSC.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24); remainder unverified

### 10. `inurl:` to find duplicates; `site:` for related pages and indexed count
**Lecture ref:** 004, 009, 011 | **Old way:** `site:domain` to view indexed pages; `inurl:` to find parameter duplicates.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Outdated as a primary method.
**Better way:** site:/inurl: counts are approximate and unreliable for audits (Google has long said so; unverified in the fetched sources). Use GSC Page indexing report + URL Inspection API (quota ~2,000/day/property: from memory, unverified), sitemap-level indexing comparison, and a crawl vs index diff in Python/BigQuery. Keep site: only for quick spot-checks and internal-link idea mining.
**New tools/skills:** GSC bulk export to BigQuery; Python + URL Inspection API; gsc-mcp / mcp-seo-audit servers with Claude Code (community servers, vet before use).
**Sources:** https://pypi.org/project/gsc-mcp/ (community, 2026); https://glama.ai/mcp/servers/ahonn/mcp-server-gsc (community)

### 11. Internal links: "pass ranking power", bounce rate, funnel
**Lecture ref:** 011, 012 | **Old way:** Internal links reduce bounce rate -> better ranking; links share authority.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Mixed.
**Better way:** Bounce rate as a ranking mechanism is an unsupported claim (Google has repeatedly said it doesn't use GA metrics; no source fetched here, unverified). Defensible reasons for internal links: discovery, context/anchor-text signals, and user journey. Improve the method: use Screaming Frog Crawl Analysis for orphans (kept), but also use link-graph metrics (Link Score / inlink counts), click depth, and compare to GSC impressions to find high-demand pages with weak internal support; and Screaming Frog 24.0 (19 May 2026) added "Find Uncrawlable Links" (links Google can't follow, e.g. JS onclick links without href), relevant because Google follows `<a href>` only. Orphan detection needs sitemap + GSC + analytics sources connected, not just the sitemap. Redirect chains: fix internal links to final 200 URL (kept; correct).
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog 24.x (Find Uncrawlable Links, Auto Compare Crawls); Sitebulb/Ahrefs Site Audit link-graph views; Python networkx for PageRank-style internal link analysis; claude-seo `seo-cluster` for link matrices.
**Sources:** https://www.screamingfrog.co.uk/seo-spider/release-history/ (fetched 2026-10-24); bounce-rate claim: unverified

### 12. URL length "115 characters" and "under 60 chars ideal"
**Lecture ref:** 014 | **Old way:** Top-ranking URLs average under 60 chars; Screaming Frog flags "Over 115 characters"; users trust URL more than title/description.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ The 115 threshold is a Screaming Frog default, not a Google rule. The "people believe the URL more" research claim is unverified.
**Better way:** Google's URL-structure page gives no length limit; it recommends readable words over long IDs, hyphens over underscores, minimal parameters, percent-encoding non-ASCII, and notes URLs are case sensitive (convert to one case). Correlation studies of URL length are weak causal evidence. Practical rules: keep URLs human-readable and stable; don't chase a character count; prioritise (1) one canonical lowercase form, (2) no unnecessary parameters, (3) no changing URLs without 301s. Google mostly shows breadcrumb-style paths instead of the raw URL on mobile SERPs (observation, unverified), reducing the "URL as CTR factor" argument. Non-ASCII is not a defect: IDN/UTF-8 URLs (e.g. Arabic, Thai) are fine if percent-encoded consistently; the lecture's flag-as-error approach would wrongly flag legit non-English slugs for the course's multilingual audience.
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog URL tab filters (adjust the 115 threshold as a configurable); Python regex/urllib audit for case/dup variants.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/url-structure (fetched 2026-10-24)

### 13. JavaScript SEO: the "5-second render limit"
**Lecture ref:** 016, recap | **Old way:** "Googlebot has roughly a 5-second time limit to render JavaScript"; Walmart missed content after the window.
**Verdict:** ❌ Myth.
**Better way:** The 5 s figure came from Martin Splitt (Chrome Dev Summit 2019) as the MEDIAN time a page spends in the render queue (90th percentile: minutes), not a render timeout. Dave Smart (Tame the Bots) tested in 2026: API responses delayed 6-12 s were still included in Google's rendered DOM, because Google's WRS uses a virtual clock it can pause (SEJ, 28 Jul 2026, secondary reporting of community test; not an official Google statement). Google's own JS doc gives no timeout number and says rendering happens "when resources allow," queue "a few seconds, but it can take longer." Correct teaching: the real risks are (a) render-queue delay, (b) rendering skipped for non-200 pages (Google's Dec 2025 update: non-200 pages "might" not be rendered), (c) resources blocked by robots.txt, (d) content requiring user interaction/scroll, (e) other bots (many AI crawlers and social scrapers do not execute JS), so put critical content in the initial HTML. The Walmart "+40% organic revenue via Next.js SSR" story is unverified (no source found); don't cite as fact.
**New tools/skills:** GSC URL Inspection live test (rendered HTML + screenshot + JS console errors); Chrome DevTools "disable JavaScript" test; Screaming Frog JS rendering + "JavaScript" tab (rendered vs raw diff); Playwright scripts; claude-seo `seo-technical` JS rendering checks.
**Sources:** https://www.searchenginejournal.com/google-seo-test-shows-what-happens-in-5-second-rendering-window/583878/ (2026-07-28, secondary); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/javascript/javascript-seo-basics (2026-10-24); https://www.seroundtable.com/google-render-5sec-28532.html (secondary); https://www.seroundtable.com/google-render-non-200-code-40627.html (secondary, Dec 2025)

### 14. Dynamic rendering / Prerender.io, noscript, lazy loading
**Lecture ref:** 017 | **Old way:** If SSR isn't possible, use dynamic rendering such as Prerender.io; add `<noscript>` versions; lazy-load issues solved by noscript.
**Verdict:** ❌ Dynamic rendering is discouraged; ⚠️ noscript is not Google-recommended.
**Better way:** Google's dynamic-rendering page is a workaround, "not a long-term solution"; recommended: server-side rendering, static rendering/SSG, or hydration (SEL coverage 2022; Google page flagged). It's still allowed (not cloaking if content is substantially the same) and prerendering is still defensible for non-Google bots as a stopgap. Google's JS-basics page does not mention `<noscript>`; Google renders JS, so the right fix is the content being in rendered HTML or, better, initial HTML. Lazy loading: lazy-load images (native `loading="lazy"`), never above-the-fold/LCP; never hide indexable text behind scroll/click events. Also from the Dec 2025 doc update: don't inject noindex via JS, set canonical in HTML, return correct status codes (soft-404 handling for SPAs via redirect to a real 404 URL or noindex on error pages). Use framework defaults: Next.js App Router Server Components/SSG/ISR, Nuxt, Astro, Angular SSR.
**New tools/skills:** Next.js/Nuxt/Astro SSR/SSG; Screaming Frog JS rendering; WebPageTest/Lighthouse; Playwright raw vs rendered diff scripts; Cloudflare/Vercel edge rendering. WP Rocket is a performance plugin; it does not fix JS indexing (the lecture's WP suggestion conflates speed with crawlability).
**Sources:** https://searchengineland.com/google-no-longer-recommends-using-dynamic-rendering-for-google-search-387054 (secondary, 2022); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/javascript/javascript-seo-basics (2026-10-24)

### 15. Hreflang: "country code uppercase", methods, "11 mistakes"
**Lecture ref:** 019, 020 | **Old way:** Language lowercase, country UPPERCASE as a "strict rule"; hreflang URLs must match canonicals; HTML/sitemap/HTTP header.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Case rule is wrong as stated; the rest mostly correct.
**Better way:** Google: "The hreflang value is case-insensitive; Google accepts lowercase and uppercase codes" (the course's own example uses `en-us` lowercase, contradicting its rule). ISO 639-1 language + optional ISO 3166-1 Alpha-2 region confirmed; region cannot stand alone (`be` = Belarusian, not Belgium); codes like `es-419`, `EU`, `UN`, and `UK` don't work (UK must be `GB`). Return links required ("if two pages don't both point to each other, the tags will be ignored"); x-default for unmatched users (language selector pages). Google does not use hreflang or the HTML `lang` attribute to detect page language; localized pages with translated main content are not duplicates. Add: Bing relies on `lang`/content-language more than hreflang (common knowledge, unverified here); don't mix methods (conflicts); hreflang is a signal not a directive and doesn't geo-restrict. Audit at scale in Screaming Frog (hreflang tab) or with Python + advertools; for large sites the sitemap method (xhtml:link) is easier to maintain. The lecture's "11 mistakes" actually lists 10: one is missing.
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog hreflang crawl analysis; Sitebulb hreflang hints; Ahrefs Site Audit; claude-seo `seo-hreflang` skill; custom Python reciprocity checker.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/specialty/international/localized-versions (fetched 2026-10-24)

### 16. Structured data: types taught (FAQ, Review, Event...) and CTR claims
**Lecture ref:** 022, 023, recap | **Old way:** FAQ schema shows expandable Q&A in SERPs; types Product/Recipe/LocalBusiness/Article/FAQ/Review/Event; "CTR up to 30%", "15-30% organic traffic in 2-3 months", structured data needed for voice/AI search.
**Verdict:** ❌ FAQ rich results are gone; ⚠️ other claims unsupported.
**Better way:** Google stopped showing FAQ rich results on 7 May 2026; the FAQ search-appearance filter, rich result report and Rich Results Test support were scheduled for removal in June 2026 and the Search Console API support in August 2026. FAQPage markup is harmless but yields no visible result. HowTo rich results were removed in 2023, and FAQ was already limited to authoritative gov/health sites since Aug 2023. Several types were retired in 2025 (Course Info, ClaimReview, Estimated Salary, Learning Video, Special Announcement, Vehicle Listing; one source says Book Actions status unclear) per secondary reporting. Google's search gallery today lists 25 types (Article, Breadcrumb, Carousel, Course list, Dataset, Discussion forum, Education Q&A, Employer aggregate rating, Event, Image metadata, Job posting, Local business, Math solver, Movie, Organization, Product, Profile page, Q&A, Recipe, Review snippet, Software app, Speakable, Subscription/paywalled content, Vacation rental, Video). Teach the current priority: Product + merchant listing, Organization, Breadcrumb, Article, LocalBusiness, Video, JobPosting, Event, Profile page. The "up to 30% CTR" and "15-30% traffic" figures and the 2.3%->3.1% case are anecdotal and unverified. For AI search, Google says no special schema, llms.txt or AI files are needed; schema must simply match visible text.
**New tools/skills:** Schema.org Validator (validator.schema.org) for generic syntax; Rich Results Test for eligibility (check availability post-FAQ change); GSC Enhancements reports; schema generation from CMS/template code (not copy-paste per page); claude-seo `seo-schema` skill.
**Sources:** https://www.searchenginejournal.com/google-drops-faq-rich-results-from-search/574429/ (2026-05-10, quotes Google's doc notice); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/appearance/structured-data/search-gallery (fetched 2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/appearance/ai-features (2026-10-24); 2025 retirement list: https://www.getpassionfruit.com/blog/what-changed-with-google-drops-faq-rich-results-and-what-to-do-now (secondary, unverified)

### 17. Schema implementation: JSON-LD in `<head>`, AI-generated markup, "penalty" framing, required properties
**Lecture ref:** 023, 024 | **Old way:** Paste JSON-LD in `<head>`; use Gemini/ChatGPT to generate; schema mismatch = "penalized"; Product needs review/aggregateRating/offers.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Mostly right, framing imprecise.
**Better way:** Product snippet rule confirmed: need `name` plus one of review, aggregateRating or offers; offers alone gives a warning. But: (a) merchants should use merchant listing markup (priceCurrency etc.) and place Product markup "in the initial HTML for best results": JS-injected Product markup can make Shopping crawls "less frequent and less reliable". (b) JSON-LD may be placed in head or body; it need not be in `<head>`. (c) Mismatch with visible content leads to rich-result ineligibility or a structured-data manual action, which is not an algorithmic "penalty" on rankings; the course's "Recipe schema -> Google penalized the site" anecdote is unverifiable. (d) Don't hand-prompt LLMs per page: generate JSON-LD from CMS fields in templates, validate in CI (JSON schema / Rich Results Test API-less workaround: headless run) and monitor in GSC. (e) Use `@graph` with stable `@id` entities (Organization, WebSite, Person) to connect entities across pages.
**New tools/skills:** Yoast/Rank Math schema graph; Next.js JSON-LD component; Claude Code with schema docs in context and a validator in the loop; Screaming Frog structured data validation (kept, enable JSON-LD extraction).
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/appearance/structured-data/product-snippet (fetched 2026-10-24)

### 18. Audit tooling and AI-assisted workflows
**Lecture ref:** 003, 004, 009, 012, 017, 020, 023 | **Old way:** Screaming Frog (manual clicks) + paste screenshots into Gemini/ChatGPT; "AI deep search for the last 10%"; "technical skills are harder to replace with AI".
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Outdated workflow; salary/AI-job-security claims unsourced.
**Better way:** Screaming Frog SEO Spider 24.0 (19 May 2026) shipped an official "Screaming Frog SEO Spider MCP", plus Auto Compare Crawls and crawl change summaries; community MCP servers also exist (e.g. screaming-frog-mcp on PyPI, v0.3.3 May 2026; most features need a paid license). Senior workflow: scheduled headless crawls (CLI) -> export CSVs -> Claude Code/Python analyses (orphans, canonical conflicts, hreflang reciprocity, redirect chains, indexability) -> ticket-ready findings with prioritisation by GSC clicks/impressions; connect GSC (Search Analytics + URL Inspection API, owner permission required) and server logs for crawl-vs-index truth; add post-deploy SEO regression checks (robots.txt, canonicals, noindex, sitemap, status codes, schema) in CI. Always verify AI output against raw data: LLMs hallucinate directives (e.g. suggest crawl-delay/noindex-in-robots.txt which Google doesn't honour). Also check Sitebulb and Ahrefs Site Audit for visual hints/scheduling, which the lecture never names. Compensation figures in Lecture 001 are unsourced; treat as unverified.
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog 24.x MCP/CLI; GSC MCP servers (gsc-mcp, mcp-seo-audit: community, vet before granting access); advertools; Playwright; claude-seo skills (seo-audit, seo-technical, seo-drift, seo-google, seo-sitemap, seo-hreflang, seo-schema, seo-geo); Sitebulb; Ahrefs Site Audit.
**Sources:** https://www.screamingfrog.co.uk/seo-spider/release-history/ (fetched 2026-10-24); https://pypi.org/project/screaming-frog-mcp/ (community); https://pypi.org/project/gsc-mcp/ (community)

### 19. Crawl budget (referenced but under-taught)
**Lecture ref:** 003, 004, 012 | **Old way:** Broken links and short changefreq "waste crawl budget"; crawl budget is a general concern.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ Overstated for small sites.
**Better way:** Google's guidance targets sites with 1M+ pages updating weekly, 10k+ pages updating daily, or many "Discovered - currently not indexed" URLs (rough estimates). Most sites in the course audience (the lecture itself says 80% of benefits come from basics on small sites) have crawl-demand/quality problems rather than budget limits. Real levers: remove low-value URL spaces (faceted/infinite), return proper 404/410, fix soft 404s, keep server fast (crawl rate rises with healthy responses), accurate lastmod, consolidate duplicates; do not use noindex or robots.txt expecting reallocation.
**New tools/skills:** GSC Crawl stats report; log-file analysis; Screaming Frog Log File Analyser.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/large-site-managing-crawl-budget (fetched 2026-10-24)

---

### Still correct (no objection)
- Sitemap limits (50 MB / 50,000 URLs), absolute URLs, only indexable canonical URLs, sitemap index for large sites, `Sitemap:` line in robots.txt, GSC submission.
- Robots.txt controls crawling not indexing; path case-sensitivity; GSC robots.txt report; specific/longer-path precedence.
- Hyphens over underscores, consistent lowercase, minimal parameters, 301 on URL change, avoid redirect chains (Google URL-structure page).
- Product schema "one of review/aggregateRating/offers" requirement; test markup before deploying.
- Hreflang: reciprocal links, self-reference, x-default, ISO codes, sitemap method for large sites.
- Self-referencing canonicals, absolute canonical URLs, single canonical, canonical in HTML head (Google prefers HTML-set canonical).

### Priority fixes for the retelling (ranked)
1. Replace the "5-second render limit" with the queue-median explanation (Obj. 13).
2. Remove priority/changefreq and rewrite lastmod guidance (Obj. 1).
3. Mark FAQ rich results as ended (7 May 2026) and refresh supported-types list (Obj. 16).
4. Fix hreflang case rule (Obj. 15).
5. Reframe canonical as strong signal not directive; add 301-vs-canonical decision (Obj. 7, 8).
6. Add AI-crawler/robots.txt matrix and Google-Extended scope (Obj. 5).
7. Replace dynamic rendering recommendation with SSR/SSG/hydration (Obj. 14).
8. Add Screaming Frog 24 MCP + GSC API workflow (Obj. 18).
