# SECTION 15 — Core Web Vitals & Page Speed (Optimizing for User Experience & Rankings)

**Lectures:** 001 Why site speed impacts rank · 003 What is LCP · 004 Find your LCP bottleneck (Chrome DevTools) · 005 Quick LCP wins (images & fonts) · 006 Advanced LCP fixes (server & resources) · 007 Taming third-party scripts · 009 Understanding INP · 010 Boost responsiveness (audit scripts & platform tips) · 011 JavaScript & element tuning for INP · 013 Understanding CLS · 014 Fix layout shifts · 016 Common speed mistakes that backfire · 017 Preventing speed regression · 019 WordPress speed tips · 020 Shopify speed tips · 021 Wix & Squarespace speed tips · 022 Custom-site speed tips · 023 The 80/20 speed rule.
*(Numbering gaps 002, 008, 012, 015, 018 have no transcripts.)*

---

## 🎬 Lecture 001 — Why Site Speed Now Directly Impacts Your SEO Rank (Google's Rules)

**Core Web Vitals (CWV)** = specific metrics Google uses to measure **the actual user experience of your site.** Traditional SEO factors (keywords, backlinks) look at content quality and site authority; **CWV focus purely on how users interact with your site — how it feels to use.**

- Google **officially made Core Web Vitals ranking signals in 2021** → they directly affect how pages rank.

🧠 **Analogy — a nice physical store.** CWV are the key things that make your shopping trip smooth and enjoyable. **Three metrics right now:**

| Metric | Question it answers | Store analogy |
|---|---|---|
| **LCP — Largest Contentful Paint** | How quickly does the **main content** load and become visible? | Walking in and **seeing the main products right away** |
| **INP — Interaction to Next Paint** | How **responsive** is the site when someone clicks, taps or types? | Getting help from an assistant **the moment you need it** |
| **CLS — Cumulative Layout Shift** | How **stable** is the layout while loading — does stuff jump around? | **Store shelves not moving** while you reach for something |

**Why Google cares:** its job is to give searchers the best results. If someone clicks a result and the page is slow, buttons don't respond, or the layout keeps shifting, **people get frustrated and leave → a negative signal back to Google.** So Google prioritizes sites with **relevant content *and* a smooth, fast, stable experience.**

**The tiebreaker:** two sites with similar content quality and comparable backlinks, both relevant → **often the one with better CWV gets the edge.** Especially important for **very competitive keywords** where small ranking gains mean lots of traffic/business. Studies (Searchmetrics, per the lecture) found **top-ranking sites consistently have significantly better CWV than lower-ranked sites.**

### 📈 Proof it works (case studies)
- **Vodafone** improved **LCP → +8% sales.**
- **Wix** found users who optimized for CWV saw **significantly more organic traffic** than those who didn't.
- **Agrofy** (e-commerce; spelled "Agrophy" in the transcript) drastically improved **CLS → +76% conversions.**
> Consistently, improving CWV leads to **better rankings, more traffic, longer time on site and higher conversion rates (leads, sales, signups).** "Google and your users will reward you."

---

## 🎬 Lecture 003 — What Is LCP? (Why Slow Main-Content Loading Hurts)

**LCP measures how quickly the main content of your page becomes visible** — from when someone starts loading the page to when they see the **biggest, most important piece of content on screen.**

🧠 **Analogy:** you sit in a restaurant waiting for the menu; LCP is how long you stare into space before you can see it. People make **snap judgments online, literally in milliseconds**; they expect something useful fast, "not a blank white screen, not a half-loaded page." LCP measures **that critical first impression.**

### What counts as the LCP element?
Usually the **biggest thing visible without scrolling ("above the fold" — the first viewport)**:
1. A **big hero image or banner**
2. A **video thumbnail** displayed prominently
3. A **large block of text** — the main headline or first big paragraph
4. Sometimes a **background image** loaded via CSS that fills a large area

### 🎯 Thresholds (Google's targets)
| Rating | LCP time |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Good** | **≤ 2.5 seconds** |
| ⚠️ **Needs improvement** | **2.5 – 4 seconds** |
| ❌ **Poor** | **> 4 seconds** |

To **pass** the Core Web Vitals test for LCP, **at least 75% of your recorded visitors** must experience a good LCP. Poor LCP → people bounce → hurts traffic, engagement, sales/leads.

### 4 main culprits of slow LCP
1. **Slow server response times** — a slow host delays everything ("building a house on shaky foundations").
2. **Large, unoptimized images** — especially e-commerce and blogs; heavy image files are often the LCP element.
3. **Render-blocking resources** — **CSS (style files) or JavaScript (interactivity files)** that stop the browser from displaying anything until they finish loading.
4. **Slow resource load times** — even with a fast server and nothing blocking, a **huge LCP file** (massive image, complex script, video) takes long to download, especially on slow mobile connections.

> 🎯 Improving LCP is not just ticking a box for Google — it gives visitors a better, faster, less frustrating experience → more engagement and conversions.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 004 — Find Your LCP Bottleneck (Using Chrome DevTools, Step-by-Step)

Before fixing a slow LCP, you must know **which specific element** is the largest contentful thing Google measures — hero image? main headline? something else? Identifying it is "a crucial first step."

### 🛠️ Chrome DevTools method
1. Open your website in **Google Chrome**.
2. **Right-click anywhere → Inspect.** A panel opens (docked bottom or side) = **DevTools**.
3. Click the **Performance** tab (among Elements, Console, etc.).
4. Click the **reload button inside the Performance panel** (the circular arrow near the top-left of the panel — *different from the browser's reload button*). Chrome reloads the page while analyzing everything; a timeline fills with colorful bars/network requests. "Don't get overwhelmed — let it finish loading."
5. Below the main chart, find the track/row of **key performance metrics** and find the marker labeled **LCP** (possibly alongside CLS or INP markers).
6. **Click the LCP marker** → it selects that point in the timeline and opens details in the **Summary** tab at the bottom of the Performance panel.
7. In the Summary tab find the line **"Related Node"** and **hover (no click needed)** over it → DevTools **highlights the element on your actual web page** that is the LCP element. 🎯 *That's the element to optimize first.*

**Extra info in the Summary tab when LCP is selected:** a **breakdown of phases** — **time to first byte, resource load delay, render delay** — and sometimes insights/warnings (e.g. **if lazy loading might be hurting LCP discoverability**).

### 🔎 Alternative: Google PageSpeed Insights
Enter your site's address; the report also names your **LCP element**, usually under **Diagnostics**. Less interactive for finding the element than DevTools, but excellent for **your overall LCP score and specific optimization suggestions.**

> 🏁 **Homework:** open your own site in Chrome, run DevTools and find your LCP element with the **hover-on-Related-Node trick.** *"Knowing what you need to fix is always the first step."*

🔗 **Resources:** Google PageSpeed Insights → https://pagespeed.web.dev/ · *Tools & Links: LCP Analysis Tools (PageSpeed Insights, DevTools)* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

---

## 🎬 Lecture 005 — Quick LCP Wins (Image & Font Optimization Secrets)

Step 1 was identifying the LCP element; step 2 is fixing it. Recap of the **four culprits**: slow server response, large/unoptimized images, render-blocking CSS/JS, slow resource load times. Over this and the next lectures: **optimizing images, improving server speed, caching, CDNs, fonts, third-party scripts ("a big one"), preloading JavaScript, handling CSS.** This lecture = the **low-hanging fruit: images and fonts.**

### 🖼️ Images (often the LCP element — hero images/banners)
1. **Use modern formats.** Forget old JPEG/PNG if you can → **WebP**: files **~25–35% smaller** with the same look. There's an even newer, smaller format **AVIF**, but **older browsers don't support it** — if you have an older audience, stick to WebP. Smaller files = faster download = better LCP.
2. **Resize before uploading.** Don't upload a camera photo for a small thumbnail. **If an image displays at 500 px wide, upload a 500 px version, not 4,000 px.**
3. **Use responsive images** — different sizes for different screens (one for mobile, one for desktop) so users download only what they need.
4. **Compress** (reduces file size, ideally without visible quality loss): online tools like **TinyPNG** or **Squoosh** (the transcript spells it "Scrooge") before uploading. In **WordPress**, plugins like **Smush, ShortPixel or Optimole** (transcript: "Optimal") can compress **and convert to WebP automatically** on upload.
> 📈 *Real result:* Arun improved an e-commerce site's LCP by **1.2 seconds** just by **converting the main banner to WebP and sizing it correctly for mobile.*

### 🔤 Fonts
Web fonts look good but are files that must download and can slow LCP.
1. **Load only what you use.** Fonts come in weights (light, regular, bold) and styles (italic). *Does your site really need 10 variations of the same font?* Many themes load all by default → **load only the specific weights/styles you actually use** (e.g. regular, bold, maybe italic).
2. **Add `font-display: swap`** where you define web fonts (inside **`@font-face`**) — "a crucial piece of CSS." It tells the browser to **show text immediately using a standard backup font (e.g. Arial) and swap in the fancy web font once it has downloaded.** Without it, the browser may wait for the font before showing any text — "terrible for LCP" if the text is part of the largest content.
3. **Consider self-hosting Google Fonts.** With Google Fonts the browser must connect to Google's servers; **hosting font files on your own server can be faster.** Some WordPress performance plugins offer a one-click option.

> These image and font tweaks are "low-hanging fruit" — relatively easy with significant LCP wins. Next: server speed and render-blocking resources.

🔗 **Resource:** *Tools & Links: Image Optimization Tools & WordPress Plugins* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

---

## 🎬 Lecture 006 — Advanced LCP Fixes (Server Speed & Resource Handling)

More powerful (sometimes slightly more technical) fixes, still targeting the four causes (slow server, big images, render-blocking resources, slow resource loading).

### 1️⃣ Quality hosting — "your server is the foundation"
If it's slow to respond, **everything else is delayed.** **Cheap shared hosting is often slow.** If the site matters, invest in better hosting such as a **VPS (virtual private server)** or **cloud hosting** — they give **dedicated resources** so your site isn't dragged down by "noisy neighbors." Look for hosts known for speed (e.g. cloud-based).

### 2️⃣ Caching
🧠 **Analogy:** baking a cake — caching is **showing a picture of the cake to everyone** instead of baking a new cake every time. Three kinds:
| Type | What it does |
|---|---|
| **Page caching** | The server normally runs code, pulls database data and assembles the page on every visit. Page caching does that **once, saves the finished page**, and delivers it instantly to the next visitor. |
| **Browser caching** | Tells visitors' browsers to **save certain files (logo, design files) on their own computer**, so on the next page they're loaded from local storage instead of downloaded again. |
| **Object caching** | Like page caching but **specifically for database information** — big speed-up for database-heavy sites like WordPress. |
Most good hosts offer some caching; **WordPress users can use plugins like WP Rocket or W3 Total Cache**, often combining page + browser caching.

### 3️⃣ CDN (Content Delivery Network)
A CDN **stores copies of your files (images, CSS, JavaScript) on servers all around the world.** Visitors download them from the **server geographically closest** to them → shorter distance, faster downloads. **Cloudflare** is a popular CDN and many hosts integrate it.

### 4️⃣ Preload the LCP resource
Tell the browser what's most important. If you know your **main hero image is always the LCP element**, give the browser a heads-up with **`<link rel="preload">`** in your HTML: *"you're definitely going to need this image for the most important content, so start downloading it now."* Some performance plugins automate this.

### 5️⃣ Minimize render-blocking resources
**CSS:** identify the **minimum ("critical") CSS needed to style content visible above the fold** and **inline it directly in the HTML** (not as a separate file); load the rest of the CSS **non-blocking, later.** "Complex but very effective" — performance plugins often automate it.
**JavaScript:** avoid loading large JS files early if not immediately needed; use **`defer`** or **`async`** on script tags:
| Attribute | Behavior | Good for |
|---|---|---|
| **`defer`** | Downloads **alongside other things** but **waits to run until the main HTML has been parsed** | Scripts needing the full page structure |
| **`async`** | Downloads and **runs as soon as possible without waiting for HTML parsing** (downloaded and rendered simultaneously with HTML, "like a multi-core processor") | Independent scripts like **Google Analytics** |
> 🔑 Don't let non-essential JavaScript block the rendering of main content. Move scripts to the **bottom of the HTML** or use `defer`/`async`.

🔗 **Resource:** *Services & Plugins: Hosting, Caching Plugins & CDN Recommendations* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

---

## 🎬 Lecture 007 — Taming Third-Party Scripts (That Kill Your Site Speed)

**Third-party scripts** = bits of code (almost always JavaScript) you add to your site that **come from other companies/services**:
- **Google Analytics** (visitor tracking), **Facebook Pixel** (ad conversion tracking), **chat widgets** like **Intercom**, **ad network code** like **Google AdSense**, **social sharing buttons/feeds**, **heat-map tools** like **Hotjar**, **marketing pop-ups / notification bars**.
They add features/tracking but at a cost.

### Why they hurt performance and LCP
1. **Bandwidth competition:** every script from every company must be downloaded, slowing the download of your own content (e.g. your LCP image). 🧠 *"Too many cars on a narrow road."*
2. **Render-blocking:** some scripts insist on loading/running before the page can display → they directly delay LCP.
3. **CPU cost:** running JS takes effort from the user's device; more scripts = less processing power left for building and displaying the page.

### 🔍 Find the third-party scripts on your site (Chrome DevTools → Network tab)
1. Open your site in Chrome → **right-click → Inspect.**
2. Click the **Network** tab; make sure **recording** is active (red circle; if gray, click it).
3. **Reload** (F5 / Cmd+R) — the list fills with everything downloaded (HTML, CSS, images, fonts, JS).
4. Look at the **Domain** column: **your domain = first-party**; others like **Google Analytics, connect.facebook.net, static.hotjar** = **third-party.**
5. Use the filter bar → click **JS** to see only JavaScript; check each file's **size and load time** to find the heaviest/slowest. This gives you a clear inventory.

### 🛠️ What to do
1. **Audit:** for each third-party script ask *"Do we really need this? Is the value worth the performance hit?"* (a heat-map tool installed years ago and never viewed; a social share widget with almost no clicks). **If it isn't clearly valuable, remove it — the easiest win. Fewer scripts = faster site.**
2. **For essential scripts (e.g. analytics),** load them without blocking rendering:
   - **`defer`** — download now, run after the main page is parsed ("often the best option").
   - **`async`** — if the script can run independently whenever it finishes downloading.
   - **Load later if possible** — trigger scripts only **after user interaction.**

> Managing third-party scripts is **ongoing**; being mindful about what you add and how makes a huge difference to CWV. *That's LCP in a nutshell:* what it is, why it matters, and fixes from images/fonts to servers/scripts.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 009 — Is Your Site Unresponsive? (Understanding INP)

**INP — Interaction to Next Paint** = the second key user-experience metric; it's about **how responsive your website feels.**

**What it measures:** the time between a user's action (**clicking a button, tapping a link on a phone, typing in a form**) and **the next time the screen updates visually.**
🧠 **Analogy:** you click **"Place order"**; INP is the delay before you see the **spinning icon or "order processing" message** — or, for "Add to cart," until **the cart icon updates / the item appears in the mini-cart.** The shorter the delay, the smoother the site feels.

### FID → INP
Google **officially replaced First Input Delay (FID) with INP as a Core Web Vital in March 2024.** **FID measured only the delay on the very first interaction.** **INP looks at *all* interactions during a visit and reports the slowest one (or close to it)** → a far more complete picture of overall responsiveness.

### 🎯 INP thresholds (milliseconds)
| Rating | INP |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Good** | **≤ 200 ms** |
| ⚠️ **Needs improvement** | **200 – 500 ms** |
| ❌ **Poor** | **> 500 ms** (half a second — very noticeable) |

To **pass** the CWV assessment: **good INP (< 200 ms) for at least 75% of recorded visits.**
**Why 200 ms?** Research: delays under 200 ms **feel practically instant**; above that people notice lag; above 500 ms they get frustrated — **click again thinking the first click failed, or assume the site is broken.**

### 💥 How poor INP hurts the business
1. **Increased bounce rates** — laggy interactions frustrate users, signals poor quality.
2. **Abandoned shopping carts** — if every checkout button takes ages, they buy elsewhere.
3. **Damaged trust** — slow, clunky = unprofessional/unreliable; makes users hesitant to engage or give information.
> 📈 *Case:* a business cut INP from **600 ms to under 200 ms** → **conversion rate +15%.**

### 🔎 Check your INP
**Google PageSpeed Insights** (`pagespeed.web.dev`) → enter your URL → look at the **INP score in the Core Web Vitals section.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 010 — Boost Site Responsiveness (Audit Scripts & Platform Tips)

The **biggest, quickest INP wins** come from **reducing the work the browser has to do** when someone interacts. **Most common cause of slow interactions: too much code — especially JavaScript — running unnecessarily.** Everything added (WordPress plugins, Shopify apps, third-party scripts for analytics/ads/chat/social) adds code that competes for browser attention and processing power.

### 🧹 Step 1 — a ruthless audit
Go through everything installed or embedded: *"Is this absolutely essential? Is its value worth slowing every interaction for every user?"*
- **Deactivate and delete** plugins you don't truly need.
- **Remove apps** that aren't critical.
- **Cut third-party scripts** that don't offer significant benefit.
- "Be brave. Less is often more." Arun has seen INP improve **by over 100 ms** just by removing a few heavy unnecessary plugins/scripts — "often the most impactful first step."

### 🧩 Platform-specific tips
**WordPress**
- **Plugin audit is key** — review active plugins.
- Themes and page builders add code → consider **lightweight themes.**
- Use performance plugins wisely: **WP Rocket or Perfmatters** can **delay JavaScript execution** — stops non-essential scripts from loading **until the user starts interacting (scrolling, moving the mouse)** → frees the browser initially. ⚠️ **Test carefully — delaying the wrong script can break things.**

**Shopify**
- Each app adds code → **regularly review installed apps.**
- Use **Shopify's own speed report** to see whether specific apps cause slowdowns.
- **Remove unused or low-value apps.**
- Use performance-optimized themes like the default **Dawn** theme.
- Check theme settings and **disable fancy animations/effects if not crucial** (they rely on JavaScript and can hurt INP).

**Custom-coded sites**
- Talk to your developer about INP; **review the JavaScript being loaded** — can any scripts be removed?
- Ensure non-essential JS loads with the **`defer`** attribute so it doesn't block early interactions (see the advanced-LCP lecture).
- Look at **interactive elements — complex menus, filters, animations** — can they be **simplified or coded more efficiently** to reduce JS workload during interaction?

🔗 **Resource:** *WordPress Plugins for Improving INP (Script Optimization)* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

> 🎯 First big step for better INP: **reduce the unnecessary workload** — audit, remove bloat, use platform-specific tools/settings. Next lecture: tuning the code and elements that remain.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 011 — Make Clicks Instant (JavaScript & Element Tuning)

You removed the bloat — but if some interactions still feel sluggish, go deeper: optimize **the specific code and elements involved in interactions.**

### 1️⃣ Optimize JavaScript execution
JS is essential for interactivity but **often the main cause of INP delays**: after you click, the browser must process and run JS **before** it can show the result.

**Use `defer` and `async` correctly (critical for INP too):**
- **`defer`** — download the script, **wait to run until the main HTML is parsed**; generally great for interactivity scripts so they don't block initial load or early interactions.
- **`async`** — downloads and runs **whenever ready**, potentially **interrupting other things**; fine for independent scripts (e.g. some analytics) but **can contribute to INP issues if it runs during a user interaction.**
- **Key:** scripts not needed for an immediate interaction must not interfere with ones that are. Work with your developer or use performance-plugin settings to apply these correctly.

### 2️⃣ Minimize main-thread work
🧠 **Simplified:** the browser has **one extremely busy main worker** that does *everything* — runs JS, handles clicks, updates the screen, manages layout. If a piece of JS gives it **a long uninterrupted task**, it's tied up and **can't respond to your click** → **that delay is bad INP.**
**Solution:** **break long JavaScript tasks into smaller pieces** so the worker gets little breaks to check *"did the user click anything?"* and respond fast. *(Doing this is usually a developer job — restructuring code — but understanding why it helps is important.)*

### 3️⃣ Simplify interactive elements
Sometimes it's the **element's HTML structure or CSS** that makes interactions slow; more complexity = more browser work.
**How a beginner can spot complexity:**
- Look at **menus, forms, buttons, filters, sliders** — visually complex, many moving parts/animations on interaction? Slower/less smooth than simpler elements elsewhere?
- Optionally use DevTools (**right-click → Inspect**) to glance at HTML — **deeply nested `<div>` upon `<div>`** or very long complicated CSS rules may signal complexity (hard to be sure without expertise).
**Your role = identification and communication:**
- Pinpoint the specific slow/complex element (main navigation menu? product filter? a specific form?).
- Report clearly to the developer, e.g. *"The main menu animation feels sluggish when opening on mobile. Could we investigate if it's impacting INP?"* / *"This filtering widget seems complex and takes a moment to update. Can we check its performance?"*
- Sometimes suggest a simpler approach, e.g. *"Could we use a **standard browser drop-down** instead of the custom animated one to see if responsiveness improves?"*
- **Focus on key conversion points** — **Add to Cart, Submit, Checkout** buttons: even small delays have a big impact; flag perceived slowness promptly.

### 4️⃣ Optimize media (yes, for INP too)
Large images and complex video players need memory and processing power; a browser already struggling with heavy media has fewer resources to respond to clicks. So **keep compressing images with modern formats and host videos externally (YouTube/Vimeo)** — reduces overall strain and indirectly helps INP.

> 🎯 Better INP = look at what the browser does right after an interaction: **cut unnecessary scripts → optimize remaining JS (especially minimize main-thread work) → flag complex elements to developers → keep media light** — "give the browser the breathing room to respond instantly."

---

## 🎬 Lecture 013 — Stop Annoying Page Jumps (Understanding CLS)

**CLS — Cumulative Layout Shift** = the 3rd Core Web Vital; it measures the **visual stability** of your site — how much elements **move around unexpectedly while the page loads.**

🧠 You've felt it: you go to click a button/link and *boom* — an image loads above it or an ad pops in, pushing everything down, and you click something completely different. That's a **layout shift**.

### 🎯 CLS thresholds (a score)
| Rating | CLS score |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Good** | **< 0.1** (very stable) |
| ⚠️ **Needs improvement** | **0.1 – 0.25** ("a bit wobbly") |
| ❌ **Poor** | **> 0.25** (very unstable, frustrating) |
To **pass**: CLS **< 0.1 for at least 75% of visitors.**

### 💥 Why layout shifts hurt business
1. **Increased bounce rates** — people accidentally click the wrong thing, get frustrated, leave.
2. **Lost conversions** — e.g. someone aims for **Add to Cart**, the button moves, they click **Remove item** → a lost sale.
3. **Damaged trust** — jumpy pages feel unprofessional/broken.
> 📈 *Case:* an e-commerce site fixed CLS (stopped the Add-to-Cart button from jumping as the product image loaded) → **conversion rate +18%.**

### 🔍 Main causes of layout shifts
1. **Images without dimensions** (a big one) — if you don't give the browser the **width and height** in code, it doesn't know how much space to save, so the loaded image pushes content away.
2. **Ads, iframes and embeds** — ads, embedded videos, social widgets load after the main page; if no space is reserved they cause shifts.
3. **Dynamically injected content** — **cookie consent banners, newsletter pop-ups** that appear after initial load and push content down.
4. **Web fonts causing reflow** — a custom font slightly bigger/smaller than the fallback font changes text size and forces layout to readjust.

### 🛠️ See layout shifts on your own site (Chrome DevTools)
1. Open your site in Chrome → right-click → **Inspect** (DevTools).
2. Click the **three vertical dots menu** in DevTools → **More tools → Rendering.**
3. In the Rendering panel tick **"Layout Shift Regions."**
4. **Reload the page** — as it loads, **blue boxes flash** over elements that shifted unexpectedly → a visual map of the instability.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 014 — Fix Layout Shifts (Images, Ads, Fonts & Dynamic Content)

### 🖼️ Images (the #1 cause)
**Always add `width` and `height` attributes** on every `<img>` tag — "the absolute key for preventing image-related CLS." Example: `width="600" height="400"` tells the browser *"save a box exactly this big before the image even downloads"* → no guessing, no collapsing space, no shifts.
> 📈 *Case:* an e-commerce site's CLS went from a **poor 0.22 to a good 0.05** just by adding width/height to all product images. ("You should have seen my face glowing up at that time. It was fun.")

**Responsive images — aspect-ratio boxes:** images that resize for mobile vs desktop still need reserved space. A common CSS technique: create a **container `<div>` around the image** that **maintains a specific aspect ratio (e.g. 4:3 or 16:9) using CSS padding**, and the image inside fills the container → space scales correctly on all devices. (Often your team/builder handles it; good to know the principle.)

### 📢 Ads and embedded content (YouTube videos, social feeds, ads — "notorious shift culprits")
- **Reserve fixed space.** Measure the typical size of your ads/embeds, create a **container `<div>`** and use CSS to give it a specific **min-height and min-width** matching them — *"drawing a box and saying: hey, ad, you can only appear inside this box."* It acts as a placeholder so the ad doesn't collapse then suddenly expand the space. Knowing this helps you know what to ask a developer or which settings to look for in your ad network.
- **Position carefully.** Place ads/less critical embeds **below your main interactive elements or below the initial fold**; **keep important buttons and links away from areas where third-party content may load in.**

### 🔤 Web fonts
- **Use `font-display: swap`** (from the LCP lecture): show text immediately in a fallback font, then swap in the custom font → minimizes the jarring reflow.
- **Match fallback fonts:** pick a standard system fallback with **similar dimensions** to your custom font so the shift at swap time is far less noticeable.

### ➕ Dynamically injected content (cookie banners, notification bars, related-post sections)
- **Main rule: avoid inserting new content *above* existing content unless triggered by a user action** (e.g. clicking a button).
- For banners that must appear, **reserve space from the start** with a placeholder `<div>` of fixed height (just like an ad) so the banner fills the space instead of pushing content down.

### 🧩 Platform-specific tips
- **WordPress:** performance plugins like **WP Rocket / Perfmatters** often **automatically add missing image dimensions** and have **font optimization** features (applying `font-display: swap`). For **lazy loading**, they usually **exclude above-the-fold images automatically** or let you exclude the first few/specific images. If your plugin only has global lazy loading, check if you can **disable it for crucial top-of-page images** or choose a plugin with better controls.
- **Shopify:** choose **performance-oriented themes** (they often handle image dimensions better); use Shopify's built-in image lazy loading, **but disable it for critical above-the-fold images like your main product image**; **be mindful of where apps inject content.**
- **Custom-coded sites:** developer should ensure **all images have width and height**, **reserve space for dynamic content**, use **`font-display: swap`**, and apply **`loading="lazy"` only to images clearly below the initial viewport.**

> 🎯 Tackle images, ads, fonts and dynamic content → "a much smoother, more professional and less frustrating experience."

---

## 🎬 Lecture 016 — Avoid Common Speed Optimization Mistakes (That Backfire)

Even well-intentioned optimizations can backfire. **The biggest pitfall:** fixing one metric without thinking about how it affects others, or applying a technique everywhere without considering context.

### ❌ Mistake 1 — Lazy-loading everything
You learned lazy loading (images load only when about to enter the screen) improves initial load. So you apply it to **all** images — including the **hero image at the top, probably your LCP element.** Result: **your LCP score gets worse, because that crucial image is now deliberately delayed.** So common that **Google specifically warns against it.**
**Fix:** **lazy-load only images *below* the initial fold; let critical above-the-fold content load immediately.**

### ❌ Mistake 2 — Plugin and tool conflicts (especially WordPress)
You install a caching plugin, then an image-optimization plugin, and your theme also has built-in speed features → **multiple tools all trying to do similar things (optimize JS/CSS, cache files) fight each other** — one modifies a file, another modifies it again, breaking things or canceling benefits.
> *Real case:* a site used **three optimization tools at once**; **untangling the mess made the site faster than when all were active.**
**Fix:** **choose ONE primary performance plugin** (e.g. **WP Rocket, Perfmatters**) and learn its settings well. If you use other tools (separate image optimizer, CDN), **disable overlapping features in your main plugin.**

### ❌ Mistake 3 — Forgetting about performance regression
You optimize for hours, scores look fantastic, you celebrate — then weeks/months later scores have tanked. **Performance regression** happens when:
- **No ongoing monitoring** — nothing checks scores regularly.
- **No performance budget/guidelines** — so things inevitably get added: **content editors upload huge uncompressed images; developers add heavy-JS features; marketing adds yet another tracking script or heavy pop-up tool.** Each seems minor, but they add up and erode gains.
**Fix:** establish a **performance budget** — simple rules such as **maximum image file sizes**, or **approval before adding new third-party scripts**; create a **simple checklist** for anyone updating the site; set up **basic monitoring** (even just checking PageSpeed Insights or Search Console regularly).

---

## 🎬 Lecture 017 — Keep Your Site Fast (Preventing Speed Regression)

Regression happens easily as new content, scripts or images are added. To protect performance, be **proactive and consistent**:

1. **Monitor regularly (start simple).** Even a **monthly check-in with Google PageSpeed Insights** or the **Core Web Vitals report in Search Console** catches problems early. For automation later: the free **webpagetest.org** can **schedule tests and alert you if things slow significantly.** "Starting simple is fine — the key habit is consistency."
2. **Be mindful of what gets added.**
   - Keep **a simple list of the third-party scripts running on your site and why**; **review it every few months** — *Do I still use this tool? Is it worth its presence?* If not, remove it. "Decluttering scripts is a great habit."
   - **Images (a huge one):** set a **non-negotiable rule** — **always run new images through a compression tool like TinyPNG or Squoosh before uploading** (transcript spells it "Sqooze").
3. **Share the knowledge.** With colleagues, clients, freelancers (or just yourself), connect performance to **real-world metrics — better user experience, lower bounce rates, higher conversions, better search visibility.** When everyone understands *why*, small habits (compress images, think twice before adding a script) become a **culture of performance.**

> 🎯 Building these checks and balances into your regular workflow ensures your optimization effort **lasts** and protects your progress long-term.

🔗 **Resource:** *Tool: WebPageTest.org for Performance Monitoring* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

---

## 🎬 Lecture 019 — WordPress Speed Tips (Plugins, Themes & Builders)

WordPress powers a huge chunk of the internet; its flexibility means you must be smart about setup. Three key areas:

### 🔌 1. Plugins
Plugins give WordPress its superpowers but are **"hands down the most common reason WordPress sites slow down"** — every plugin adds code → more load time.
- **Recommendation:** use a good, well-supported **performance plugin** that bundles **caching, file minification, lazy loading, database cleanup.**
  - **WP Rocket** — popular **premium** option; effective and user-friendly; costs money but often saves hours of manual tweaking.
  - **Budget route (free):** a common combo is **Autoptimize** (transcript: "Auto Optimize") — great at **combining and shrinking CSS/JavaScript files** — plus a solid caching plugin such as **WP Super Cache or W3 Total Cache.** ⚠️ Free route often requires more learning and careful configuration.
- 🔁 **From the mistakes lecture — don't install multiple plugins that do the same job.** **Don't run two caching plugins; don't run two JavaScript optimizers** — they'll clash, break things or slow the site further. **Choose your primary tools (one for caching, maybe one for file optimization) and disable overlapping features in other plugins or even your theme settings.** "Keep it clean."

### 🎨 2. Theme choice (crucial)
The theme controls the entire look, feel and structure — "a heavy foundation makes for a slow house." **Lightweight is usually right.** Themes coded for speed: **GeneratePress, Cadence, Astra** (transcript: "Astro") — fantastic starting points that avoid loading tons of extra code/features.
> 💡 *"Switching from a slow, bloated theme to a well-coded lightweight one can sometimes improve your Core Web Vitals more dramatically than almost any other single change."*

### 🧱 3. Page builders — power with responsibility
**Elementor, Divi, Beaver Builder (transcript: "Bevo Builder"), Bricks** offer drag-and-drop design and **can** build fast sites, but they add extra code layers for visual editing, so it's easier to build heavy pages. Key:
- **Be efficient** in how you build sections.
- **Use their built-in performance settings** (many now have them).
- **Optimize images aggressively.**
- **Pair them with a good performance plugin** — be more proactive.

> 🎯 For WordPress: **manage plugins wisely, choose your theme carefully, build efficiently.**

🔗 **Resource:** *Plugins & Themes: Recommended Performance Plugins & Lightweight Themes* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed

---

## 🎬 Lecture 020 — Shopify Speed Tips (Themes, Apps & Images)

Shopify handles servers, security and checkout, but **you still control things that affect CWV**: themes, apps and images.

### 🎨 Themes
- Shopify's own default themes, like the current **Dawn**, are generally built with performance best practices → a solid reliable starting point.
- Browsing the theme store: look for themes whose descriptions say **fast / performance-optimized / lightweight.**
- 🔑 **Vital tip:** *before installing or buying a theme, find its **live demo store**, copy the demo URL and run it in **Google PageSpeed Insights**.* This shows the theme's baseline performance — "don't just trust the marketing copy, test the demo."

### 📦 Apps
Apps add features (loyalty programs, review widgets, upsells, advanced search…) but **almost every app adds code (usually JavaScript) to the storefront → potentially slower loading and interaction.** Be disciplined:
- **Once a quarter** review installed apps: *Is it critical? Does it generate a clear ROI? Is the functionality worth the performance hit?*
- Use Shopify's built-in report: **Admin → Analytics → Reports → Web performance** — it analyzes your store speed **relative to similar Shopify stores** and can sometimes highlight apps contributing to slowdowns (a starting point, not always definitive).
- **If an app isn't essential, isn't really used, or its value is questionable — uninstall it.** "Fewer apps almost always mean a faster store — often the quickest win."

### 🖼️ Images
Shopify serves images through a fast global **CDN**, but you can help. (More technical — potentially for your theme developer.) Shopify's template language **Liquid** lets you **request specific image sizes**; your theme should request **appropriately sized images for each context** (small thumbnail vs. large main product image) — far more efficient than a huge image the browser shrinks. It also **helps prevent layout shifts (CLS)** because the browser knows image dimensions sooner.

> 🎯 For Shopify: **choose your theme wisely, be disciplined about apps, make sure the theme leverages Shopify's image system correctly.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 021 — Wix & Squarespace Speed Tips (Making the Most of Built-In Tools)

These platforms are famous for ease of use; the trade-off is **less direct control over deep technical performance levers** than WordPress. **That doesn't mean you can't optimize CWV** — focus on what you *do* control.

### 🟦 Wix
- Wix has put more focus on performance and offers tools inside the platform.
- **Enable Wix's built-in image optimization features** — let Wix help with image compression.
- Check the **Site Speed dashboard** in your Wix admin panel — it often gives **specific, actionable recommendations tailored to Wix.**
- **Use visual effects sparingly:** scroll-triggered animations and parallax backgrounds look neat, but overdoing complex animations/effects adds work for the browser, **potentially hurting responsiveness (INP) and smoothness.** Keep it clean.

### ⬛ Squarespace
- Some templates are inherently simpler and lighter — **when choosing/updating a template, lean toward cleaner, less complex designs if speed is a priority.**
- **Don't overload pages** (especially the homepage) with too many content blocks, galleries or sections — **fewer elements = faster load.**
- **Disable built-in features you don't need** (announcement bars, promotional pop-ups) unless used for a critical business reason — "every bit of code removed helps."

### 🏆 The #1 thing on both platforms: image optimization *before* uploading
- **Always resize images to approximately the dimensions they'll be displayed at**, then **run them through a compression tool like TinyPNG, Squoosh ("SQOOS" in the transcript) or similar** before uploading. "Often the single most impactful thing you can do to improve LCP and overall load times on these platforms."

> 🎯 Even on Wix/Squarespace: **clean design, mindful features/effects, use built-in tools, and be meticulous about image optimization.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 022 — Custom Site Speed Tips (Working With Your Developer)

Custom-built sites (via a developer or agency) give **full control over code and server** — the upside — but **performance isn't handled automatically; it must be built in and actively managed.** You don't need to be a developer, but you need to know **what to ask.**

### ❓ Questions/topics to raise with your developer
1. **Make CWV part of the discussion** — *"Achieving good Core Web Vitals scores is a priority for us"*; discuss **LCP, INP, CLS targets.**
2. **Hosting** — is the server environment optimized? does the plan have enough resources?
3. **Backend performance** — is server-side code (**PHP, Python, Node.js**, etc.) efficient? **Are database queries optimized?** *(Slow server response kills LCP.)*
4. **Clean, efficient HTML, CSS and especially JavaScript** — bloated code is a major source of poor INP and LCP.
5. **JavaScript loading strategies** — using **`defer` or `async`** appropriately for non-critical scripts? If the site uses **React, Vue or Angular**, are they following that framework's performance best practices?
6. **Preloading critical resources** — the main LCP image, key font files.
7. **Critical CSS** — inlining the essential styles for above-the-fold content to speed initial render. (Advanced, but important for custom builds.)
8. **Testing** — thorough performance testing during development and before launching new features; encourage regular use of **Lighthouse (built into Chrome DevTools)** or **WebPageTest** to catch regressions before they affect users and CWV scores.

> 🎯 Performance is **a team effort**: you set the goals, your developer implements the technical solutions — keep communication open.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 023 — The 80/20 Speed Rule (Focus on What Matters Most First)

After tips for WordPress, Shopify, Wix, Squarespace and custom builds — *where do you begin when time and resources are limited?* → the **80/20 rule (Pareto principle): ~80% of results come from ~20% of effort.** Don't tackle every tiny technical detail first.

### 🔑 The crucial 20% (regardless of platform)
1. **Images** — **properly size images before upload, choose modern formats like WebP (the transcript says "VIP"), and compress effectively.** "Often yields massive LCP and overall speed wins. Make this non-negotiable."
2. **Bloat** — **be ruthless about removing unnecessary stuff**: every extra WordPress plugin, every non-essential Shopify app, every third-party script added ages ago. **Less code and fewer external requests ≈ faster site.** *Audit and remove. Be brave.*
3. **Simplicity** — is your theme overly complex, loading features you never use? Heavy animations/visual effects you don't need? Can layouts be streamlined with fewer complex elements? **A cleaner, simpler design inherently performs better and is easier to maintain.**

> 🎯 Focus intensely on **images, bloat, and simplicity** first — likely **80%+ of the total possible improvement.** Nail the basics, build momentum, see real results; **only then worry about finer technical details.** "The smartest way to begin your optimization journey."

---

## ✅ Section 15 Recap

- **CWV = ranking signals since 2021:** **LCP** (main content speed — good ≤ 2.5 s / poor > 4 s), **INP** (responsiveness — good ≤ 200 ms / poor > 500 ms; replaced FID in March 2024), **CLS** (visual stability — good < 0.1 / poor > 0.25). Pass = **75% of visits** good. Tiebreaker for similar sites. Case studies: Vodafone +8% sales (LCP), Agrofy +76% conversions (CLS), INP 600→200 ms = +15% conversions, CLS fix = +18% conversion.
- **LCP causes:** slow server, big images, render-blocking CSS/JS, big resources. **Find the element:** DevTools → Performance → reload → LCP marker → Related Node hover (or PageSpeed Insights). **Fixes:** WebP/AVIF, resize, responsive, compress; limit font weights, `font-display: swap`, self-host fonts; VPS/cloud hosting; page/browser/object caching; CDN (Cloudflare); `<link rel="preload">`; critical CSS inlining; `defer`/`async`; tame third-party scripts (Network tab → JS filter; remove, defer, async, load on interaction).
- **INP fixes:** ruthless plugin/app/script audit; WP Rocket/Perfmatters delay JS (test carefully); Shopify apps report & Dawn theme; correct `defer`/`async`; break up long main-thread tasks; simplify interactive elements & flag slow conversion buttons to devs; light media.
- **CLS fixes:** `width`/`height` on every image; aspect-ratio containers; reserve space for ads/embeds/banners; `font-display: swap` + matched fallback fonts; don't inject content above existing content; DevTools → Rendering → Layout Shift Regions.
- **Backfire mistakes:** lazy-loading everything (incl. LCP image), overlapping optimization plugins, no monitoring/performance budget (regression).
- **Prevent regression:** monthly PageSpeed/Search Console CWV checks, webpagetest.org schedules, script inventory reviewed every few months, compress every new image, share the "why".
- **Platform tips:** WordPress (one performance plugin; GeneratePress/Cadence/Astra; page builders carefully), Shopify (Dawn, test demo in PageSpeed, quarterly app audit, Web performance report, Liquid image sizes), Wix/Squarespace (built-in image optimization, site speed dashboard, sparing effects, simple templates, pre-optimize images), custom (talk CWV targets, backend, JS loading, preload, critical CSS, Lighthouse/WebPageTest).
- **80/20:** images → bloat → simplicity.

**🔗 Section resources:** Section Notes → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/notes-core-web-vitals-page-speed · Hub (tools, plugins, hosting/CDN, themes, WebPageTest) → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/core-web-vitals-page-speed · PageSpeed Insights → https://pagespeed.web.dev/


> 🧐 **How to read the 🔄 blocks:** everything above this line is the course *as taught* (kept unchanged). The block below is an independent *objector* review (researched 2026-10-24) saying where the old way is outdated, wrong or beatable, and what a senior practitioner would do instead. Items marked *unverified* rest on secondary sources. A second pass re-checked the highest-impact claims against primary sources; its corrections are shown in the 🔬 box at the top of each affected block, and the full evidence is in the final appendix.

## 🔄 Objector's Review — Better / Updated Way (Section 15)

> 🔬 **Second-pass verification (primary-source check, 2026-10-24) — corrections to the review below:**
> - **WordPress core vs. plugin:** WordPress 6.5 AVIF support and 6.3 automatic `fetchpriority` are **confirmed**; but "WordPress converts uploads to WebP/AVIF by default" is **not** a core default (that is the *Modern Image Formats* plugin).
> - **Screaming Frog price:** official page shows **$279/yr** (the course's ~$259 is outdated); free limit **500 URLs** confirmed. **PageSpeed Insights "field data first"** is not stated in the docs — unverified.
> - **Confirmed:** Core Web Vitals thresholds (LCP 2.5 s, INP 200 ms, CLS 0.1 at the 75th percentile), Lighthouse 13 Insights, Speculation Rules, bfcache.

**1. Lecture ref:** 001, 003, 009, 013 (metrics and thresholds)
- **Old way:** LCP ≤2.5s, INP ≤200ms, CLS <0.1, pass at 75% of visits. FID was replaced by INP in March 2024.
- **Verdict:** ✅ Still correct. Add one detail: assess mobile and desktop separately, and a page passes only if all three metrics meet their targets.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Read the p75 split by device.
  - web.dev says stable CWV metrics change at most once a year, with advance notice.
  - Treat blog claims of a "2026 LCP 2.0s threshold" as unfounded. Several 2026 third-party posts say thresholds did not change.
- **New tools/skills:** CrUX / PSI field section; the `claude-seo:seo-google` skill pulls CrUX history.
- **Sources:** https://web.dev/articles/vitals (page last updated 2024-10-31); https://prooflytics.io/blog/core-web-vitals-thresholds-2026-lcp-inp-cls (third party)

**2. Lecture ref:** 001 ("CWV are a ranking signal / tiebreaker / top sites have better CWV")
- **Old way:** Google made CWV a ranking signal in 2021, and it works mainly as a tiebreaker.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Overstated.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Google's docs say CWV are used by the ranking systems. They are not a standalone switch.
  - The docs say a good CWV report does not guarantee top rankings.
  - They warn against chasing a perfect score for SEO alone.
  - Non-CWV page-experience aspects do not directly help ranking.
  - Sell speed on UX and conversion. Do not promise rankings.
  - "Tiebreaker" is an interpretation, not Google wording (unverified).
  - The case studies (Vodafone, Agrofy and others) are web.dev/vendor stories. Cite them as business impact, not ranking proof.
- **New tools/skills:** `claude-seo:seo-technical`.
- **Sources:** https://developers.google.cn/search/docs/appearance/page-experience?hl=en (Google doc, accessed 2026-10); https://developers.google.cn/search/docs/appearance/core-web-vitals?authuser=0

**3. Lecture ref:** 004 (find the LCP element and bottleneck), 016 (PSI/Lighthouse reading)
- **Old way:** DevTools Performance panel, Related Node hover, PSI Diagnostics.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Start from field data (CrUX via PSI's top section, or the CrUX API). Lab runs confirm and debug; they do not define pass/fail.
  - Use the DevTools Performance panel and the "Interactions" track, which splits input delay, processing and presentation delay.
  - For LCP, work through the sub-parts: TTFB, resource load delay, resource load duration, element render delay. The course mentions TTFB, load delay and render delay, but not load duration.
  - Install the `web-vitals` library to collect your own RUM, including attribution and LoAF entries.
  - Lighthouse 13 (late 2025) replaced many old audits with "Insight" audits. Old audit keys are removed from the report and JSON.
  - Scores are unchanged, but any script that reads old audit IDs will silently get nothing.
  - Secondary sources report Lighthouse 13.x; verify the exact version.
- **New tools/skills:** Chrome DevTools live metrics (details unverified); `web-vitals` JS library; `claude-seo:seo-google` (PSI v5 + CrUX); `claude-seo:seo-performance` agent.
- **Sources:** https://developer.chrome.com/blog/lighthouse-13-0; https://developer.chrome.com/blog/moving-lighthouse-to-insights; https://www.sitepoint.com/core-web-vitals-2026-fix-interaction-to-next-paint/ (third party)

**4. Lecture ref:** 005 (images: "WebP; AVIF is not supported by older browsers, so stay on WebP"), 023
- **Old way:** Use WebP. Avoid AVIF for older audiences.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Outdated.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Core WordPress added AVIF upload and use in 6.5 (Feb 2024 dev note). The host's image library (Imagick or GD) must support it.
  - Serve AVIF with a WebP or JPEG fallback through `<picture>` or `srcset`. Browser support is broad, though a SiteGround KB note on limited Safari support may be dated, so test.
  - Dev-note claim of up to ~50% smaller than JPEG is a vendor/core claim.
  - The format helps only if the file is right-sized. Use `srcset` and `sizes`.
  - Squoosh as a manual step does not scale. Automate at upload or at the CDN.
- **New tools/skills:** `claude-seo:seo-images` (WebP/AVIF conversion checks); Squoosh CLI; WordPress core AVIF.
- **Sources:** https://make.wordpress.org/core/2024/02/23/wordpress-6-5-adds-avif-support/ (2024-02-23)

**5. Lecture ref:** 006 (preload the LCP resource), 014/016 (lazy-loading mistake)
- **Old way:** `<link rel="preload">` for the hero image. Do not lazy-load above the fold.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Correct but incomplete.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Make the LCP image discoverable in the initial HTML. Do not inject it with JS or CSS-only background if avoidable.
  - Add `fetchpriority="high"` to the one LCP `<img>`. Never `loading="lazy"` on it.
  - Preload only when the image is hidden from the preload scanner (CSS background or JS-injected). When you preload, add `fetchpriority="high"` to the preload too.
  - web.dev warns that high priority on more than one or two images defeats the point.
  - WordPress core since 6.3 auto-adds `fetchpriority="high"` to the first large non-lazy image (over 50,000 px², filterable). Audit that it picked the real LCP element.
  - Framework components (for example Next.js `Image`) lazy-load by default, so set the priority prop on the hero.
- **New tools/skills:** DevTools Performance LCP sub-parts; WP Performance Lab plugin (unverified for this exact feature).
- **Sources:** https://web.dev/articles/optimize-lcp; https://web.dev/articles/fetch-priority; https://core.trac.wordpress.org/changeset/56037 (June 2023)

**6. Lecture ref:** 005, 014 (fonts: `font-display: swap`, matched fallback, self-host)
- **Old way:** `font-display: swap`, load fewer weights, self-host Google Fonts.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete. (No primary web.dev font page was retrieved, so the items below are standard practice, partly unverified.)
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Preload the one or two critical font files (WOFF2, same-origin, with `crossorigin`).
  - Use metric-matched fallbacks (`size-adjust`, `ascent-override`, `descent-override`) so the swap does not shift layout. The course says "similar dimensions" but gives no method.
  - Consider `font-display: optional` for non-critical text. Prefer variable fonts and subsetting.
  - Cloudflare Fonts exists as a one-click option. A 2026-06-18 changelog entry only changed its error handling; it was not deprecated.
- **New tools/skills:** Fontaine / Capsize-style fallback generators (unverified); OMGF (course).
- **Sources:** https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-18-cloudflare-fonts-error-handling-security/

**7. Lecture ref:** 007 (third-party scripts: `defer`/`async`, remove, load on interaction), 010
- **Old way:** Audit scripts, then defer or async them. Delay until interaction.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete, and one claim is misleading.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - `defer`/`async` fix render blocking (LCP) but do not stop a script from running long tasks later and hurting INP. Lecture 011 partly says this.
  - Measure with LoAF-based attribution (`web-vitals` attribution build) to find the offending script and origin. Taboola's web.dev case study used LoAF and reported INP gains up to 36% (vendor case study).
  - Break up work with `scheduler.yield()`. It falls back to `setTimeout` where missing; support is Chromium-based.
  - Move tags to server-side GTM where feasible. Google's March 2025 notes say the Google tag can use service workers to feed server-side GTM.
  - Partytown (web worker) is an option for GTM-style tags. No deprecation was found, but it is fragile for tags needing DOM access. Maintenance status is unverified; check the repo.
  - Facade heavy embeds (YouTube, chat).
  - Consent banners add INP cost too.
- **New tools/skills:** Server-side GTM; Partytown; Perfmatters Script Manager; `web-vitals` attribution.
- **Sources:** https://web.dev/case-studies/taboola-inp; https://ppc.land/google-tag-now-leverages-service-workers-to-enhance-data-collection/ (third party, 2025); https://www.sitepoint.com/core-web-vitals-2026-fix-interaction-to-next-paint/

**8. Lecture ref:** 009-011 (INP)
- **Old way:** "Case: 600→200ms = +15% conversions." Slowest-interaction framing. Fix by removing plugins and breaking long tasks.
- **Verdict:** ✅ Mostly correct. Incomplete on diagnosis.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - INP is about p75 of the page's worst (near-worst) interaction per visit. Split it into input delay, processing time and presentation delay, then fix whichever phase dominates.
  - Large DOM or heavy CSS recalculation drives the presentation delay. Lecture 011's "simplify elements" covers this vaguely.
  - Field INP was healthy for most sites in Q1 2026 per a third-party dataset (p75 109ms over 43,351 sites, about 94.7% passing). Treat as unverified third-party, but it means CLS and LCP are usually the harder fixes.
  - Test on mid-tier mobile with CPU throttling, because lab INP is only a proxy.
- **New tools/skills:** DevTools Interactions track; `web-vitals` v4+ with LoAF entries (Chrome 123+).
- **Sources:** https://www.sitepoint.com/core-web-vitals-2026-fix-interaction-to-next-paint/; https://www.corewebvitals.io/state-of-cwv/inp (third party)

**9. Lecture ref:** 013-014 (CLS)
- **Old way:** `width`/`height` on images, reserve space for ads and banners, `font-display: swap`.
- **Verdict:** ✅ Correct.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Use CSS `aspect-ratio` instead of the padding hack.
  - Animate with `transform` only.
  - Do not lazy-load the LCP image and cause a late layout.
  - Gaps: bfcache restores help CLS and LCP on back navigations (see item 11). Nothing about bfcache is taught.
- **New tools/skills:** DevTools Layout Shift Regions (course); Lighthouse 13 `cls-culprits-insight`.
- **Sources:** https://developer.chrome.com/blog/lighthouse-13-0

**10. Lecture ref:** 003/006 (speed techniques list)
- **Old way:** Hosting, caching, CDN, preload, critical CSS, defer.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Missing modern navigation-level techniques.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Add Speculation Rules (prefetch or prerender next pages). WordPress core ships speculative loading from 6.8, defaulting to conservative prefetch, configurable via `wp_speculation_rules_configuration`.
  - It speeds the next page, not the entry page's LCP.
  - Core reported about a 1.9% median improvement in LCP pass rate (core proposal figure).
  - Support is limited to Chromium browsers.
  - Cloudflare Speed Brain is a similar prefetch feature, free on all plans.
  - Check pages for bfcache eligibility. Chrome finished deprecating `unload` (rollout from Chrome 146 in March 2026; completion milestone differs between cached copies of the Chrome post, 153 to 155). Remove `unload` handlers and use `pagehide`. Cloudflare Rocket Loader is reported to use `unload`, which blocks bfcache (third-party claim).
  - HTTP/3 is on by default in Cloudflare (unverified in this research).
- **New tools/skills:** Speculation Rules API; WP 6.8 core feature; Speed Brain; DevTools Application > Back/forward cache test.
- **Sources:** https://make.wordpress.org/core/2025/03/06/speculative-loading-in-6-8/; https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-speed-brain/; https://developer.chrome.com/blog/deprecating-unload/; https://web.dev/bfcache

**11. Lecture ref:** 017 (webpagetest.org schedules; monthly PSI)
- **Old way:** Monthly PSI check. WebPageTest scheduled tests. Check the Search Console CWV report.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Partly outdated.
- **Better / updated way:**
  - Monitoring should be continuous. Use RUM (`web-vitals` to GA4 or BigQuery) and CrUX History or the CrUX API.
  - Add a Lighthouse CI or PSI API budget gate in the deploy pipeline.
  - WebPageTest is now owned by Catchpoint. The free Starter tier reportedly has a monthly run cap (about 300, from aggregator sites). Verify before promising "free scheduled monitoring". Treat pricing figures as unverified.
- **New tools/skills:** CrUX API / BigQuery; Lighthouse CI; `claude-seo:seo-drift` (baseline and diff); `claude-seo:seo-google`.
- **Sources:** https://pricingsaas.com/companies/catchpoint (aggregator, unverified)

*(Numbering gaps 006, 010 have no transcripts.)*

> 📝 *Transcription note:* the speech-to-text lost some decimals (e.g. "TLS 0" = TLS 1.0, "TLS 3" = TLS 1.3). Corrections are flagged where relevant.
