# SECTION 23 — Negative SEO: Protecting Your Rankings from Sabotage

**Lectures:** 001 What negative SEO is · 002 Common negative-SEO methods (spammy links) · 003 Google & spam: an insider's view · 005 Proof negative SEO exists · 006 Recovering from brute-force attacks & hacks · 007 Analyzing your backlink profile (SEMrush) · 009 What's *not* negative SEO · 010 First line of defense: Google Search Console · 011 Protecting your best backlinks (finding & reclaiming lost links) · 013 Finding low-quality & toxic backlinks · 014 Removing toxic backlinks (outreach) · 015 Disavowing (last resort) · 016 Bad SEO practices you should never do.
*(Numbering gaps 004, 008, 012 have no transcripts.)*

---

## 🎬 Lecture 001 — Negative SEO Explained: What It Is & Why You Should Be Aware

🧠 **Analogy:** like positive and negative numbers (1, 2, 3 vs. −1, −2, −3), there's **positive SEO** and **negative SEO.** Do SEO → rankings rise; do **negative SEO → rankings fall.**

**Why learn it if you won't do it?** So you know **what search engines consider negative SEO.**
- It isn't only something done **wantedly**: **you may do things unknowingly** that fall under negative-SEO practices, causing your rankings to fall — or never rise.
- It's also something **a competitor can do to your site** to reduce your rankings — "Yes, that's possible."
- **When you don't need to worry:** if you're **starting fresh, your site isn't popular, or your niche isn't one where competitors are aware of negative SEO** (e.g. **hacking tutorials, black-hat SEO** niches), you needn't worry much about outsiders attacking you.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 002 — Common Negative-SEO Methods (Focus: Spammy Link Building)

**The most common technique competitors use: building spammy links to your site.**

📊 **Background:** suppose your site has **1,000 backlinks.** If in **24–72 hours** it gets **over 100,000 backlinks**, that **triggers an alarm for Google's Penguin algorithm** — it looks for a predefined pattern in the newly emerged links. If it decides you **manipulated** them, **your rankings for your most popular keywords will fall considerably.**

🎭 **Scenario:** you start a **smartphone news blog** following this course; after 6 months you have **100,000 daily visitors**, and rank above competitors who used to lead for **"iPhone 14"** and **"Galaxy S23."** They won't welcome that. A competitor may **pay a black-hat SEO seller to build 10,000 links overnight with a spammy anchor word on spammy websites.** If Penguin considers it manipulation by you, **you lose rankings for your best keywords** and the competitors "come up like old days."
> It may or may not happen — but yes, it *can* happen. Proof comes in lecture 005.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 003 — Google Search & Spam: An Insider's View (How Google Fights Back)

### How often Google changes
Per **Cathy Edwards, Head of User Trust – Google Search** (as cited): Google updates its algorithm **~6 times a day (>2,000 times a year)**, running **over 200,000 experiments a year** — to constantly improve relevancy, **including fighting spam websites.**

### What is a spam website? (Google's definition, as listed)
- Sites that **pretend to be or mimic the look of a well-known website.**
- Sites that **claim to run a survey but steal personal information, passwords or credit-card data.**
- Sites that **claim to offer unbiased product reviews but rewrite other sites' content using software** and earn via affiliate links.
- Sites claiming to be **a celebrity's personal website without permission**, monetized by the owner.
- In general: **any site created solely to steal search traffic by tricking engines and not useful to real visitors.**
They give users no value and are confusing or annoying. **Google doesn't just lower their ranking — it removes them from its index entirely.**

### Who decides rankings? (per **Nick Fox, VP – Google Search**)
**The people do,** not Google directly. Google learns relevance from: **how many people click a result · how many click Back immediately after visiting · how long they spend on the site.** The algorithm may initially place sites, but **rankings are eventually adjusted by real behavior on SERPs.**

### 📘 The Search Quality Evaluator Guidelines
A **168-page document** derived from people's behavior on search results, establishing **what makes a good search result** — chiefly **expertise, authoritativeness, trustworthiness (E-A-T).** *"If you want to learn why your website ranks well or why it isn't, the answer is in this 168-page document."* (Link in resources.)

### Google isn't perfect
**Eric Lehman, Senior Software Engineer at Google** (as cited), explains cases where search fails — **misinformation** and **autocomplete** — sometimes funny, sometimes affecting sentiment or provoking anger; fixing individual results isn't enough — "the tip of the iceberg" of a deeper problem → algorithm must keep evolving, **giving more importance to authority.**

### BERT / "Deeprank"
With **voice search**, people search in **natural language**, and word placement can change meaning entirely → a project led by **Elizabeth Tucker (Google product manager)** based on **BERT — Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers** — referred to here as **"Deeprank"** (*"since it uses deep learning methods by BERT, it affects ranking... and because it sounds cool"*). Examples:
- Query **"What do you preheat the oven to for cooking fish?"** — before, a page about **cookies** ranked at top; with Deeprank Google understands it's **not about fish** and doesn't show it; the answer lies in the **2nd result's description.**
- Query **"can you get medicine for someone pharmacy"** — the better result understands **"can you get medicine for someone else from a pharmacy"**; the weaker one gives a generic **"getting prescriptions filled"** result.
Earlier Google relied on behavior to adjust rankings; now Deeprank **understands the context of websites in the index.**

### 🎯 Why Arun covers this
To prepare you to **think from the perspective of both your audience and the search engineers.** *"By just learning the rules available now, you'll get stuck to what works now. If you truly understand SEO from the perspective of visitors and search engineers, you can predict what's going to happen next — or at least realize which widely used techniques can mess up rankings in the future."*

🔗 **Resource:** *Google Search Quality Evaluator Guidelines (E-A-T document)* → https://static.googleusercontent.com/media/guidelines.raterhub.com/en//searchqualityevaluatorguidelines.pdf

---

## 🎬 Lecture 005 — Proof Negative SEO Exists (Real-World Cases & Discussions)

Searching "negative SEO proof" on Google, Arun found 3 reliable results on page 1:
1. **Ker Communications** — a complete history of attacks, how they happened and how they recovered. *Their spammy backlinks increased overnight and they were penalized by Penguin.*
2. **Aqueous Digital** — *on 2nd June their spammy backlinks rose to tremendous levels; they recovered after almost 30 days.*
3. **Search Engine Roundtable** — covering a discussion on **BlackHatWorld.com**: in a poll, users said **out of 100 negative-SEO attempts only 11% failed → 89% succeeded.**
(You can replicate the search to see more.)

**Should you fight it?** *"Well, the answer is No for most cases."* Arun links a **YouTube video by Matt Cutts on negative SEO** (resources) explaining why **most websites don't need to care.** The remaining few → this section teaches **what falls under negative-SEO attacks and how to recover.**

🔗 **Resource:** Matt Cutts on negative SEO → https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HWJUU-g5U_I

---

## 🎬 Lecture 006 — Recovering from Brute-Force Attacks & Site Hacks (Security First)

**What counts as negative SEO (attacks other than links):**
- **Brute-force/hacking** your site, **deleting important files.**
- **Deleting your `robots.txt` and changing its content** so search engines are discouraged from indexing your site.

### 🛡️ Prevention
- **Basic security + firewall configuration** for WordPress.
- **Never install plugins/themes from unreliable third-party sites** — especially sites offering **premium themes/plugins for free.**

### 🚑 If you've already been hacked — the cleanup
1. **Note down your plugins and themes** (screenshot or notebook), then **delete all plugins and themes.**
2. **Reinstall them only from the official WordPress repository.** For **third-party** plugins/themes, **upload and verify them on VirusTotal.com.**
3. After reinstalling your themes, install the **Theme Authenticity Checker** plugin and **check all themes.**
4. **If you believe the site has malware or other undesirable files:** your only option is to **restore a backup from before the attack.** If you don't have one, **ask your hosting provider for support.**
   - "Backup" here = **a total server backup like R1Soft backups.** **A database-only backup would make restoration too time-consuming.**

🔗 **Resources:** WordPress plugin directory → https://wordpress.org/plugins/ · WordPress theme directory → https://wordpress.org/themes/ · Theme Authenticity Checker → https://wordpress.org/plugins/tac/ · VirusTotal → https://www.virustotal.com/

➡️ Next: tools to analyze backlink profiles.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 007 — Analyzing Your Backlink Profile for Potential Threats (with SEMrush)

*(The lecture's opening line mentions "5 different types of tools to analyze your backlink profile"; the walkthrough then focuses on SEMrush.)*

**Do you need to fight negative SEO?** — *"No for most cases."* For the remaining few, **worry about bad link building by competitors.** To find out whether a competitor harmed you, **inspect your backlink profile (do a backlink audit).**

**Why SEMrush:** "the best premium option" because it shows your backlink profile **plus a toxicity score for every backlink**, a **built-in cleanup workflow** to weed out harmful links, and **recently lost and recently acquired backlinks.**

### 🛠️ Running a Backlink Audit (demo site: newbluefx.com)
1. Enter the website → start a **Backlink Audit** campaign. Choose the scope:
   | Option | Includes |
   |---|---|
   | **Root domain** | e.g. `newbluefx.com` **including subdomains** like `shop.newbluefx.com` |
   | **www and non-www** | just those two versions (no other subdomains) |
   | **www only** / **non-www only** | only that version |
   → **Root domain is best** (covers all subdomains, www and non-www). For very large sites with many backlinks SEMrush may ask you to send an email; choose what you can and click **Start Backlink Audit.**
2. *Optional:* **Brand settings** — enter the brand name (often the domain name; some brands add extra words). **Domain categories** — SEMrush picks automatically; correct if wrong. **Target countries** — SEMrush picks the countries of your audience; uncheck any irrelevant.
3. You can **connect Google Search Console** so every backlink in your profile gets analyzed (skipped in the demo).
4. **You needn't wait** — you'll get an **email when the analysis finishes.**

### 📊 Reading the results (newbluefx.com example)
- SEMrush found **~297 toxic domains** and **426 potentially toxic domains** (may be toxic but SEMrush isn't sure) — overall toxicity score high.
- **Toxicity score per backlink:**
  | Score | Label |
  |---|---|
  | **> 60** | **Toxic (high toxicity)** |
  | **46 – 59** | **Potentially toxic** |
  | **< 45** | **Non-toxic** |
- It also showed **11 new toxic domains and 50 new potentially toxic domains.** **Initially care only about toxic backlinks/domains;** only after dealing with those, handle potentially toxic ones.
- **Pages with errors:** SEMrush found **18 pages with errors affecting ~200 backlinks** — those precious backlinks pass link juice that **never reaches the site because the pages error out.** Fix the errors and the site enjoys that link juice.
- **Priorities:** the two things to fix right away = **toxic domains/backlinks** and **error pages.** 🚦 **Red lightning symbol = deal with first; orange = next; ignore green.**

➡️ Before removing toxic backlinks, learn which situations are **not** negative SEO.

🔗 **Resource:** SEMrush → https://inspirearun.com/go/semrush/

---

## 🎬 Lecture 009 — What's *Not* Negative SEO (Interpreting Your Backlink Profile Correctly)

In the comments of most negative-SEO articles, many people claim competitors attacked them — understandable: they see an odd link, didn't make it, and conclude a competitor did. But **spammy-looking links may not be created by a competitor — or may not be spammy at all.**

### ✅ Situations that are NOT a competitor's negative-SEO attack
1. **Sites that analyze and publish info about every domain on the web** — not spammy.
2. **Directories (like dmoz.org) or traffic-information sites (like alexa.com)** also give backlinks — not spammy.
3. **Many backlinks from the *same domain* with the *same anchor text*** = your link is added **site-wide** on that site. **Check the site on Web of Trust (MyWOT.com)** — if **trustworthiness or child-safety shows green**, the backlink is safe. To check many, **install the WOT Chrome extension** and open the sites to see each one's trustworthiness.
4. **Friends/relatives** you asked to use your domain name when asked for a website.
5. **A paid SEO service from years ago** that may still be building random links.
6. **Your site got hacked** — hackers exploit **non-updated plugins/themes**, create pages on your site, redirect them to their own sites, then build spammy backlinks to those redirected pages.
> Cases 5 and 6 *do* lead to negative SEO effects, but **not by your competitor** and not with intent to make your rankings fall.

### 🚩 Signs you *are* under a negative-SEO attack
- A **sudden influx of links from spammy websites, forums, or computer-generated auto-blog posts.**
- A **sudden rise in backlinks from multiple sites using the *same anchor word that's unrelated to your domain name or niche*.**
- ☠️ **Backlinks from porn, gambling or other illegal sites** — "definitely" an attack.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 010 — Your First Line of Defense (Google Search Console Monitoring)

Free, simple monitoring in **Google Search Console (GSC):**

🛠️ **Steps:**
1. Search "search console" on Google → sign in → dashboard.
2. Click the **settings gear (user settings)** → ensure **email notifications are enabled.**
3. Pick the property you want to protect (demo: **cocktailhub.in**, a fairly new site).
4. Open **Security & Manual Actions:**
   - **Manual actions** — check for issues.
   - **Security issues** — check for **hacking attempts, malware attacks, or any security problems Google's spider detects.**
   - You must visit these pages manually; with email notifications on, you'll also be emailed. Also click the **bell icon** to see notifications about manual actions or security issues.
5. **Check backlinks Google recognizes:** expand **Legacy tools and reports → Links.**
   - Under **Top linking sites** see your backlink profile; click **More** to see all; **export to Google Sheets or download CSV.**
   - **Top linking text** = the anchor texts of your backlinks → check for **spammy anchors.**
6. 💡 You may have thousands of backlinks, but **Google shows only the ones it indexes.** *If Google doesn't show the backlinks you consider spammy, you needn't care about them.*

⏰ **Cadence:** check **External links, Top linking text and Security & Manual actions twice a month — at least once a month.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 011 — Protecting Your Best Backlinks (Finding & Reclaiming Lost Links with SEMrush)

Use SEMrush's **Backlink Analytics** tool (demo site: **newbluefx.com** — sells plugins/tools for live broadcast and post-production: title plugins, After Effects tools, LUTs/color grading; competitors e.g. **Red Giant, proDAD**).

### What Backlink Analytics shows for any site
Total **referring domains**, **backlinks**, **overall toxicity score** (if you've done the audit), the site's **Authority Score**, **increase/decrease of referring domains & backlinks over 12 months**, **new and lost referring domains/backlinks over 6 months**, **top anchor words**, **categories and authority scores of referring domains.** In the demo: **4 backlinks from sites with authority score 91–100**; most links are **text** (some image), **dofollow**, mostly from the **USA**; **similar profiles** (competitors by backlink profile); **top pages** by backlinks/referring domains.

### 🔎 Find recently lost backlinks
**Backlinks tab → Lost section.** A backlink is "lost" if it existed before but doesn't now. Example: a link from **Vegas Creative Software** — first discovered **27 Oct 2021**, last seen **26 July 2022**, lost since.

⚠️ **Verify it's really lost:** some links show as lost but are still active. Open the page (arrow icon), **copy the anchor text, press Ctrl+F on that page and paste.** Example: the **linawillow.org** link was still present (text link, **footer, site-wide**) even though marked lost.

### 🏆 Prioritize which lost links to recover
- Lost links are sorted by **descending page authority score** — higher score = higher quality.
- **Losing a good backlink from a domain with authority score above 25 is not advisable.** A backlink from a site with **authority higher than your own site's** is "highly precious — don't lose it at any cost."
- **Filter the Lost list by "follow"** — losing a **nofollow, sponsored or UGC** link doesn't matter much. You can **export to Excel**, open each link, copy the anchor, and check if it's really lost.
- In the demo there were **5,500 lost backlinks**, but most had page authority < 13; **first try to recover those with authority above 25 — fewer than 10–15 here.**

### 📧 How to contact the webmaster
1. **Find contact info:** "Contact us" link in header/footer, contact page or email. If unavailable (e.g. Vegas Creative support is customer-only), use **social media** — e.g. the company's **LinkedIn page → People section** → find the person dealing with the website (here the **site manager**) and message them.
   - If you'd **partnered** before (link created through partnership) → friendly communication. If the link was natural → an **official email** asking why it was removed or suggesting a relevant alternative.
2. **Send from your official work email address** with your website name (e.g. `admin@yourdomain.com`, `editor@yourdomain.com`) so the webmaster knows you officially represent the domain.
3. **Email template (keep it simple and short):**
   > *"I would like to compliment you on the recent change you made / the recent post you published on your website [make it genuine — mention the topic, **not** the entire title or URL, as that could be mistaken for the backlink page].*
   > *However, I was wondering if you could add back the link that you recently removed. I think it's a valuable resource for your visitors.*
   > *Thank you for your time and I hope you will consider my request. Sincerely, [your name]."*
4. **Make the webmaster's job easy:** clearly give **the URL of the page on their site, the URL of the lost link (target URL on your site), the previous anchor text (if a text link), and mention if it was an image link or other type.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 013 — Finding Low-Quality & Toxic Backlinks (SEMrush Audit Deep Dive)

*(Demo site: newbluefx.com — **126,000 total backlinks, of which 26,000 are dofollow.**)*

### 🔎 Finding the toxic ones
1. Among the **26,000 analyzed follow backlinks**, SEMrush classified **~750 as toxic.**
2. Go to **Backlink Audit → Audit section** (all 26,000 shown) → click **Toxicity score** and choose **Toxic backlinks.**
3. Filter further, e.g. **authority score 0–20** → **~523 backlinks from 222 domains**, sorted by **descending toxicity score**.
   - **Toxicity score 100** = from a highly toxic page; **80–90** = toxic but less so.

### ⚠️ Don't act blindly on the tool's score
As the site owner you must **open each backlink and check manually:** *Does it look spammy? Does the page have many external links? Is it from an illegal site? Is it from a totally unrelated site with spammy anchor words?* — then add confirmed ones to the **remove list.** *"Just because SEMrush classifies it as highly toxic doesn't mean you should right away remove or disavow."*

### 📉 Are you actually under attack? (calm perspective)
- **Losing one or two positions** for a keyword — in a day, a week, or even **page 1 → page 2** — **doesn't mean negative SEO.** The movement must be **significant:**
  - e.g. *week 1: page 1 → page 2; after two more weeks: page 10; two weeks later: page 20 — or completely out of rankings for that keyword.* **That** is when to worry.
  - Until then, check backlinks but **take no action.**
- **Having 1–2% toxic backlinks is quite common.** ~500 toxic of 26,000 dofollow = **~2%** — "not highly uncommon."

### 🏷️ Common toxic markers (quick shortcuts)
**Malicious page · non-indexed domain · de-indexed domain · community error · spam top-level domains · community warning · page comment spam · all link networks** — these are highly toxic.

### 🧪 Manual review examples (from the demo)
- Opened the first five: `mi-proav.com`, `advertise2.com`, `internet-advertisement.co.uk`, `ad-web.co.uk`, `web-advertisement.co.uk` — **three share the same URL slug "the world's most visited web pages"**; the pages provide no user value, have **immense numbers of external links and nothing else** → **absolutely spammy → remove.**
- Two others, though SEMrush rated them **100% toxic**, were **relevant, with decent content** — **even competitors (Red Giant, proDAD) have backlinks there** → *not* spammy → **don't remove.**

### ✅ Build the removal list
Select the links to remove → **Add to Remove list** (**add the entire domain** → "Move to remove").
- **Remove criteria:** page looks spammy, anchor words spammy, no value, or a **porn/gambling/illegal** site. Skip pages with useful, valuable content.
- ⚠️ **Be careful:** **remove backlinks for the specific posts/target URLs where you've seen a drastic ranking drop.** Do it for the **entire domain** *only if your entire website is affected* by negative SEO.
- Your toxic links are then accessible under the **Remove** tab.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 014 — Removing Toxic Backlinks (The Outreach Process via SEMrush)

Open **Backlink Audit → Remove tab** (your removal list). Each entry shows an **email status pipeline**: not sent → **Sent** → **Delivered** → **Read** → **Replied.**

### ✉️ Sending the removal request (demo: advertisehomepage.org)
1. Click **Send** under "Send email."
2. **Add mailbox:** connect your **professional email — NOT personal** (`@newbluefx.com`, not `@gmail`/`@outlook`) so the webmaster knows you officially represent the site.
3. **Find the webmaster's contact email:** visit the site's contact page — or use **Who.is / whois**: paste the domain, look for a contact email. If details are protected by privacy, tick the checkbox **"Please query the RDDS service of the registrar of record"** to find the email.
4. **Default subject:** *"Please remove links to our website."* SEMrush's template (auto-populated with `your project domain` and `sample backlinks`) reads roughly:
   > *"Hello, I'm working on cleaning up the website [domain] and I need your help in removing some links from your site. Your site is probably perfectly legitimate, but I'm trying to eliminate as many links as possible. It has come to our attention that some of the links to our website might have been acquired against Google's webmaster guidelines… Here are some examples [sample backlinks]… Kind regards, [your name]"*
5. Click **Send and proceed to next.**

### ⏱️ Timeline & limits
- **Daily limit: 500 emails per day** — with ~518 toxic backlinks, about **2 days** to send all.
- **Wait a week** after sending for delivery/reading/reply/removal.
- **Not removed? → Follow up:** click **Follow up** → send (use contact email or the RDDS option; add your project domain, name, mailbox).
- **If still no reply/removal after 14 days from the first email, or 7 days after the follow-up → add the link to the Disavow list:** back in the **Remove** tab → **Add to Disavow list** (add the **entire domain** — all that domain's backlinks move; e.g. one domain had 3 backlinks).
- The **Disavow tab** lets you **export to a text file** once all unremovable toxic links are collected.

> 🔑 **Why this order:** Google recommends you **first approach webmasters yourself and make every effort to remove toxic backlinks;** only if those efforts fail should you disavow.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 015 — Disavowing Links (Your Last Resort with Google's Disavow Tool)

**Disavow only the toxic links you couldn't get removed.**

🛠️ **Steps:**
1. In SEMrush, **Backlink Audit → Disavow tab → "Export to text"** → you get a **text file with just domain names** (plus any URLs you added separately).
2. Open **Google's Disavow Tool.** Google's page says (paraphrased from the screen): *"If you have a manual action against your site for unnatural links, or you think you're about to get one because of paid links or link schemes that violate our quality guidelines — **ask the other sites to remove those links. If you can't get them removed, disavow those sites using this tool."***
3. ⚠️ **Try your level best to contact the webmaster first.** *If the tool is used incorrectly, or you wrongly assume some links are toxic and upload them, **you might lose the ranking you even have now.***
4. **Select the property.** ⚠️ **Domain properties are not supported by the disavow tool** ("Domain properties are not supported at this time") — you must add your site again as a **URL-prefix property** (e.g. `https://inspirearun.com`) to upload a disavow list. (Arun had added his site both ways.)
5. Click **Upload disavow list** → select the text file from SEMrush → **Open** → it uploads and submits.
6. You can later **replace the list, download it, or *Cancel disavowals* if submitted by mistake.**

> **The whole process:** find toxic backlinks → remove via email → follow up → if still not removed, add to disavow list → export text file → upload in Search Console's disavow tool.

🔗 **Resources:** Disavow links to your site (Google help) → https://support.google.com/webmasters/answer/2648487?hl=en · Disavow tool → https://search.google.com/search-console/disavow-links · Who.is (domain contact info) → https://who.is/

---

## 🎬 Lecture 016 — Bad SEO Practices (That YOU Should Never Do!)

**If you follow any of these you'll get penalized by Google.**

1. **Thin/shallow content.** The lecture says the minimum should be **300 words per post** — long-form increases ranking chances, **but don't bargain quality just to add words.**
2. **Cloaking and doorway pages.** *Doorway/cloaking = rigging or coding your site to show one version to humans and another to search engines* — "one of the serious practices banned by Google."
3. **Keyword stuffing.** Adding the keyword more than naturally needed — e.g. **5–10 times within 100 words.** The lecture's rule of thumb: **add the exact keyword about once per 100 words** and use **related terms ("proof terms")** instead of stuffing.
4. **Multiple H1 tags.** Use **only one H1 per post;** multiple H2/H3/H4 are fine.
5. **Hidden text.** Old trick: stuff keywords hidden from users — **white text on a white background, or very small fonts.** Related to cloaking.
6. **Piracy/plagiarism.** Don't copy posts from other blogs/sites. You *can* rewrite, but **a rewrite without additional unique content adds no value.** Instead **read, understand, and write in your own words, adding your own opinion** so visitors get more value.
7. **Too many ads above the fold.** Webmasters add ads above the fold for revenue; as covered in the UX section: **no more than one ad above the fold (per this lecture), no pop-ups or interstitials.**
8. **Buying or selling paid dofollow links.** When your site reaches a certain domain authority, companies may offer **hundreds of dollars for paid dofollow link placement** — **buying or selling paid links gets your site penalized.**
9. **Link spam.** New webmasters, "in the name of link building," **submit links to multiple forums, blogs and websites without judging quality.** Those spammy backlinks **reduce your rankings instead of building authority.**

> 📝 **Consistency notes (this is an older-style lecture, so compare with newer sections):** Section 10 says there is **no magic word count** (this lecture says a 300-word minimum — treat 300 as a *thin-content floor*, not a target). Section 10/12 say **don't chase keyword density** (this lecture's "once per 100 words" is a rough guard-rail against stuffing). Section 20 cites a **3-ads-per-page** AdChoices guideline while this lecture says **one ad above the fold** — apply the stricter rule above the fold.

---

## ✅ Section 23 Recap

- **Negative SEO** = actions that lower rankings — sometimes **unknowingly by you**, sometimes **by competitors.** Low risk for new/unpopular sites or niches where competitors don't know SEO. Matt Cutts' view: most sites needn't worry.
- **Attack types:** **spammy link blasts** (e.g. 1,000 → 100,000 links in 24–72 h, 10,000 spammy-anchor links overnight → Penguin alarm), **hacking/brute force, deleting or rewriting `robots.txt`**, malware.
- **Proof:** Ker Communications, Aqueous Digital, Search Engine Roundtable/BlackHatWorld poll (89% of attempts succeed).
- **Google background:** ~6 updates/day (2,000+/yr), 200,000+ experiments; spam definitions; rankings shaped by **clicks, back-button, dwell time**; **168-page Search Quality Evaluator Guidelines (E-A-T)**; **BERT ("Deeprank")**.
- **Hack recovery:** note + delete plugins/themes, reinstall from the official repo, VirusTotal for third-party, Theme Authenticity Checker, **restore a full pre-attack server backup (R1Soft)** or call your host.
- **SEMrush backlink audit:** root domain scope; toxicity **>60 toxic / 46–59 potentially / <45 non-toxic**; fix **toxic links** and **error pages with backlinks** first (red → orange → ignore green).
- **What is NOT negative SEO:** domain-info sites, directories (dmoz/alexa-style), site-wide links (check with **WOT**), friends/relatives, old paid SEO services, hacked site. **Real signs:** sudden spammy influx, same unrelated anchor everywhere, porn/gambling/illegal sites.
- **Monitoring:** GSC → Security & Manual actions, Legacy tools → Links (top linking sites/text); check **2×/month (min. monthly)**; email notifications ON.
- **Protect good links:** SEMrush **Backlink Analytics → Lost**; filter **follow**; verify with Ctrl+F; prioritize authority >25; contact from official email with a short genuine, detail-rich request.
- **Toxic-link removal:** manually vet (1–2% toxic is normal; drastic multi-week ranking collapse is the real alarm) → remove list → outreach (≤500 emails/day) → follow up after 1 week → disavow after 14 days (or 7 days post-follow-up) → export text → upload via **URL-prefix property** in Google's Disavow Tool.
- **Never do:** thin content, cloaking/doorway pages, keyword stuffing, multiple H1s, hidden text, plagiarism, excessive above-the-fold ads, paid links (buying/selling), link spam.

**🔗 Section resources:** Section Notes → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/notes-negative-seo · SEMrush → https://inspirearun.com/go/semrush/ · Google Search Quality Evaluator Guidelines · VirusTotal · Theme Authenticity Checker · Who.is · Disavow tool (links above)


> 🧐 **How to read the 🔄 blocks:** everything above this line is the course *as taught* (kept unchanged). The block below is an independent *objector* review (researched 2026-10-24) saying where the old way is outdated, wrong or beatable, and what a senior practitioner would do instead. Items marked *unverified* rest on secondary sources. A second pass re-checked the highest-impact claims against primary sources; its corrections are shown in the 🔬 box at the top of each affected block, and the full evidence is in the final appendix.

## 🔄 Objector's Review — Better / Updated Way (Section 23)

> 🔬 **Second-pass verification (primary-source check, 2026-10-24) — corrections to the review below:**
> - 🔴 **Corrected:** Semrush's own docs do **not** use the course's per-link bands (>60 toxic / 46–59 potential / <45). Semrush reports a per-link score 0–100 and an *overall* profile risk of **High >10%, Medium 3–9%, Low <3%** toxic backlinks. Use Semrush scores only as a lead for manual review.
> - **Penguin real-time since 2016 / SpamBrain "neutralizes" spam links:** the Google posts exist (Sept 2016 "Penguin is now part of our core algorithm"; Dec 2022 link spam update) but their bodies could not be read — "real-time" and "neutralize" rest on secondary sources. The Gary Illyes line (PubCon 2019) is SEJ's **paraphrase**, not a verbatim quote.

**1. Lecture ref:** 001-002 (negative SEO exists; 1,000 -> 100,000 links in 24-72h triggers Penguin)
- **Old way:** Link blasts trigger Penguin and tank your best keywords.
- **Verdict:** ❌ Outdated/overstated.
- **Better way:** Penguin is real-time and devalues bad links (2016); SpamBrain neutralises spam links (2022). Google's reps advise to ignore such links; negative SEO via links is rare and typically ineffective. Real negative-SEO risks today: hacks/malware/injected spam pages, fake removal/DMCA requests, scraped duplicates, review bombing, crawl-budget abuse (fake URLs), robots.txt tampering, fake manual-action reports (unverified). No fixed numeric threshold exists; that "100,000 in 72h" figure is not from Google.
- **New tools/skills:** GSC Security issues, Manual actions, Crawl stats; uptime/file-integrity monitoring (Cloudflare WAF, Sucuri/Wordfence); Ahrefs/Semrush new-link alerts.
- **Sources:** [SEL-PENGUIN] 2016-09; [G-DEC22] 2022-12; Illyes "ignore" advice via https://userp.io/news/google-gives-advice-about-toxic-links/ (secondary).

**2. Lecture ref:** 003 (Google "insider's view": ~6 updates/day, Nick Fox "people decide", clicks/back-button/dwell, 168-page guidelines, "Deeprank"=BERT)
- **Old way:** Rankings are adjusted by click/pogo/dwell; guidelines = E-A-T; BERT nicknamed Deeprank.
- **Verdict:** ❌ Several outdated/incorrect points.
- **Better way:** (1) "Deeprank" is not an official name (RankBrain, BERT, MUM, neural matching are); treat as colloquial. (2) Google has repeatedly denied direct use of click/dwell as ranking signals for ranking individual pages (used in evaluation experiments), yet the 2024 leak/US v. Google testimony showed Navboost/click systems exist — nuanced, say "behavioral data is used in some systems". (3) Rater guidelines are *E-E-A-T* (Experience added 2022; "Trust" central), they are for raters and don't directly rank; the doc is now ~170+ pages and updated (check current version). (4) Spam definition list should include the three 2024 policies. (5) Don't present 2020-era talking points (Cathy Edwards etc.) as current.
- **New tools/skills:** Official spam policies page; Search Status Dashboard; Search Quality Rater Guidelines current PDF at guidelines.raterhub.com (version unverified).
- **Sources:** [G-SPAM] 2026-10-24; course resource URL (static.googleusercontent.com ... searchqualityevaluatorguidelines.pdf) — currency unverified.

**3. Lecture ref:** 005 (proof: Ker Communications, Aqueous Digital, BlackHatWorld 89% poll)
- **Old way:** Cites case studies and a black-hat forum poll as proof.
- **Verdict:** ❌ Weak evidence, dated.
- **Better way:** BlackHatWorld poll = self-reported sellers; not evidence. Best-known controlled test (Moz/Rand Fishkin 2014 style) is also old. Teach: attacks that demonstrably work are *technical* (hacks, injected pages, toxic redirects, scraped/duplicated content outranking, fake review floods). Verify any "attack" through GSC and server logs before blaming links.
- **Sources:** Illyes/Mueller guidance as above (secondary); no new primary study found (unverified).

**4. Lecture ref:** 006 (hack recovery)
- **Old way:** Delete all plugins/themes, reinstall from repo, VirusTotal, Theme Authenticity Checker, restore full server backup (R1Soft).
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Directionally fine, dated.
- **Better way:** Isolate site, rotate ALL credentials (WP admin, DB, hosting, SFTP, API keys, salts), restore clean backup (immutable/off-site, tested), update core/plugins, scan with Wordfence/Sucuri/Imunify, review `.htaccess`, `wp-config`, mu-plugins, cron, new admin users; remove injected pages/URLs and request review in GSC Security issues; check robots.txt and sitemap integrity. Theme Authenticity Checker is long unmaintained (unverified; verify). Never use nulled themes/plugins (course point is right).
- **New tools/skills:** Wordfence, Sucuri, Patchstack, Cloudflare WAF, WP-CLI `wp core verify-checksums`, `wp plugin verify-checksums`, Git-based deploys, `claude-seo:seo-drift` for robots/meta change detection.
- **Sources:** best-practice; no single fetched source (unverified).

**5. Lecture ref:** 007 & 013 (SEMrush toxicity score; thresholds >60/46-59/<45; "best premium option")
- **Old way:** Use Semrush Toxic Score thresholds to build remove/disavow lists; red/orange/green triage; 1-2% toxic is normal.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Partly wrong. Use with heavy caution.
- **Better way:** Semrush's own metric is a heuristic from 45+ markers, not Google's view; sources report different threshold cut-offs; Mueller has said (Reddit/social, secondary) that you will not regain rankings by following Semrush's report or disavowing, and to ignore such reports. Course correctly says "vet manually"; improve: only treat as relevant if (a) manual action exists, or (b) you ever bought/spammed links, or (c) a clear, huge, coordinated spam cluster appears alongside a measurable drop that coincides in time. Don't mass-email 500 removal requests/day (also an email-spam risk). Also note "Pages with errors" fix is valuable and correct: reclaim link equity via 301/restoring 404s — keep that.
- **New tools/skills:** Ahrefs/Semrush as *monitors*, not judges; log-file + GSC for traffic-drop diagnosis; date-aligned analysis with update dates from Search Status Dashboard.
- **Sources:** https://userp.io/news/googles-john-mueller-addresses-concerns-again-on-using-toxic-links/ (secondary, date unverified); https://www.thehoth.com/blog/understanding-semrushs-toxicity-score/ (vendor); Semrush toxic markers help https://sv.semrush.com/kb/965-toxic-markers-description (surfaced, not opened).

**6. Lecture ref:** 009 (what's NOT negative SEO; dmoz.org, alexa.com, WOT)
- **Old way:** Directories like dmoz/alexa give harmless links; check with Web of Trust (MyWOT).
- **Verdict:** ❌ Outdated references; ✅ the core reasoning (most odd links aren't attacks) is right.
- **Better way:** dmoz (closed 2017) and Alexa (shut 2022) no longer exist (widely known, URL not fetched = unverified). WOT is crowd-sourced reputation, not a Google signal; replace with a manual check (is the page indexed? site: query; traffic; content sanity). Site-wide footer links from one domain = one referring domain; count domains not links.
- **New tools/skills:** `site:` operator, Ahrefs traffic, Common Crawl, VirusTotal URL check.
- **Sources:** [G-LINKS] 2026-10-24 (grouping by root domain).

**7. Lecture ref:** 010 (GSC monitoring; "Legacy tools and reports → Links"; "Security & Manual Actions")
- **Old way:** Legacy tools -> Links; check 2x/month.
- **Verdict:** ❌ UI outdated; ✅ cadence OK.
- **Better way:** The Links report is now a top-level left-nav item ("Links"); Manual actions and Security issues are under "Security & Manual Actions"; keep email alerts on. Links report: not exhaustive, no nofollow flag, max 1,000 rows on screen, up to 100,000 rows in exports, not exposed in API. Weekly: Manual actions, Security issues, Page indexing, Crawl stats; monthly: Links export diff (save CSVs to build your own history, since GSC keeps no per-link history).
- **New tools/skills:** GSC (also Bing Webmaster Tools), `claude-seo:seo-google` for API pulls (not Links), scheduled CSV snapshots to a Sheet.
- **Sources:** [G-LINKS] 2026-10-24.

**8. Lecture ref:** 011 (lost-link reclamation: filter follow, authority >25, outreach)
- **Old way:** Use Semrush Lost list; reclaim authority>25 follow links; email webmaster.
- **Verdict:** ✅ Good tactic; ⚠️ nofollow doesn't "not matter".
- **Better way:** Prioritise by *referring page traffic + topical relevance + whether you can fix it yourself (301/restore page)*. Most "lost" links are caused by YOUR redirects/404s (check first). Use "lost links" alerts monthly. Nofollow/sponsored/ugc are hints, still potentially useful (referrals, brand mentions, AI-engine citations). Don't ask for restoration when link was edited out for good editorial reasons; offer an update instead.
- **New tools/skills:** Ahrefs Site Explorer (Backlinks -> Lost), Screaming Frog, Wayback, Ahrefs Alerts.
- **Sources:** [G-QUALIFY] 2026-10-24; [G-LINKS] 2026-10-24.

**9. Lecture ref:** 014 (outreach for link removal, 500/day, templates stating links "acquired against Google's guidelines")
- **Old way:** Mass-email webmasters asking to remove links, wait 7-14 days, then disavow.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Only valid in a real manual-action scenario.
- **Better way:** Google's removal-first guidance applies when you have/expect a manual action for unnatural links. Mass outreach admitting your links "might violate guidelines" can create bad records and spam-folder harm; send only for links you actually built/paid for. Document everything for reconsideration request.
- **Sources:** [G-DISAVOW] 2026-10-24; [G-SPAM] 2026-10-24.

**10. Lecture ref:** 015 (disavow steps; domain properties unsupported; URL-prefix)
- **Old way:** Export Semrush list, upload to disavow tool via URL-prefix property.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Mechanically correct, strategically over-applied.
- **Better way:** Confirmed by Google's page: "Domain properties are not supported", file is UTF-8/ASCII .txt, `domain:` prefix, max 100,000 lines / 2 MB, new upload replaces old, only owners, per-property (http/https/www variants separate), changes take weeks, disavowed links still appear in Links report, "Cancel disavowals" exists. Google: use only when considerable spam links caused or will likely cause a manual action. Otherwise do nothing. Keep a versioned copy of the file. Do NOT disavow based on toxicity score.
- **New tools/skills:** GSC Manual actions report; git for disavow file versioning.
- **Sources:** [G-DISAVOW] 2026-10-24.

**11. Lecture ref:** 016 (bad SEO practices list)
- **Old way:** 300-word minimum; one keyword per 100 words; only one H1; max one ad above the fold; paid links penalised.
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Mixed; some outdated rules of thumb. (Course itself flags the inconsistencies.)
- **Better way:** No word-count floor from Google; thin = no value. Keyword density is not a ranking tactic. Multiple H1s are allowed in HTML5 and Google has said it is fine (older Mueller statement; URL not fetched = unverified) — treat "one H1" as a clarity convention, not a penalty. Add the *current* named spam policies: scaled content abuse (incl. mass AI pages), site reputation abuse, expired domain abuse, cloaking, doorway pages, hidden text, link spam, plus AI-manipulation (blogs say Google extended spam policy to cover manipulating generative AI responses on May 15 2026 — UNVERIFIED). Paid links: valid only with `rel=sponsored`/`nofollow`.
- **New tools/skills:** [G-SPAM] checklist; `claude-seo:seo-content` for thin/AI-scale checks.
- **Sources:** [G-SPAM] 2026-10-24; https://www.techwyse.com/news/search-news/google-june-2026-spam-update-ai-search (blog, unverified).

**12. Lecture ref:** Section 23 overall (missing)
- **Verdict:** ⚠️ Incomplete.
- **Better way:** Add: negative-SEO triage decision tree (1 verify traffic drop vs. update date; 2 check manual action/security; 3 check technical (robots, noindex, canonicals, redirects, hacked URLs); 4 only then links); brand-reputation attacks (fake reviews, Reddit/AI answer poisoning, AI-answer misinformation about your brand — monitor ChatGPT/Perplexity/AI Overviews for brand claims); content scraping (DMCA, canonical self-references); recovery from spam updates is by improving/removing violating content, not disavow (a blog reports Google says disavow may not help recovery from a link spam update — UNVERIFIED).
- **New tools/skills:** Brand monitoring (Brand24, Ahrefs Brand Radar), Copyscape, Google DMCA tools, Cloudflare WAF, `claude-seo:seo-drift`, `claude-seo:seo-audit`.
- **Sources:** [G-SPAM] 2026-10-24; https://almcorp.com/news/google-finishes-september-2026-spam-update/ (blog, unverified).

---

## Where the course is still correct (confirmed)
- Google does not use Moz DA / Ahrefs DR as such; relevance first. (Sec 18/004)
- Disavow rarely needed; remove-first; domain properties unsupported. (Sec 18/006, 23/015) [G-DISAVOW]
- Paid links without qualification violate policy; avoid niche-edit buying. (Sec 19/016) [G-SPAM]
- Anchor-text variety and avoiding exact-match over-use; GSC is first stop for links. (Sec 18/008-010) [G-LINKS]
- Most negative-SEO worry is overblown; do not act on toxicity scores blindly. (Sec 23/013)

# SECTION 24 — WordPress Technical SEO: Platform-Specific Fixes & Optimizations

**Lectures:** 001 Duplicate content in WordPress (archives, tags, categories) · 002 404 error pages in WordPress · 003 Canonical tags in WordPress (Yoast & Redirection) · 004 Common canonical tag mistakes.
*(WordPress-specific companion to the generic Technical SEO section — Section 14.)*

---

## 🎬 Lecture 001 — Duplicate Content in WordPress (Fixing Archives, Tags & Categories)

### The tool: `noindex`
**Avoid duplicate content by modifying index/noindex.** A **`noindex`** page can still be **accessed/crawled** by Google but **will not be indexed**, so its URL won't appear in search results.

### Why do it?
If you don't actively manage **category, tag and author pages**, then what you see in **author archives looks more or less the same as category or tag pages** — duplicated from the homepage and each other. **Date archives** are the most worrisome.

⚠️ **When NOT to noindex (e-commerce / landing-page sites):** if you sell shoes, you want people to land on **product pages** for "Nike Air Zoom Winflo" / "Adidas Ultraboost" **and on category pages** like **"running shoes"** for "best running shoes", or **branded tag pages** like **"adidas"** for "adidas running shoes." **Noindexing categories/tags would lose those opportunities** — so don't do it where categories/tags are potential landing pages.

**For blogs / sites where you don't actively manage category, tag, author or date archives:** those pages are simply duplicated content.
> *Example (searchengineland.com):* posts like *"Google search adds new spam policy"* and *"Google publishes document on more notable ranking systems"* appear on the homepage **and** on the SEO category page **and** on an author archive (Barry Schwartz) **and** in tags. **At least they added unique lines about each category and author bios.** A **single-author site's** archive would look nearly identical to the blog page.

### ✅ If you keep category/tag pages indexable — make them worth indexing
1. **Create hierarchy** in category pages.
2. **Don't use two tags for singular/plural** (e.g. *loafer* and *loafers*) — pick one and stick to it.
3. **Write a detailed description** of what each category/tag is about, with **100–300 words of unique content**, and optionally **FAQs** on frequently asked questions (use Google's **People Also Ask** or **SEMrush question keywords**).

**Benefit of noindexing poorly managed archives:** you **increase the indexed unique-content ratio** of your site.

### 🛠️ Do it in Yoast SEO (Search Appearance → Archives)
(Assumes Yoast is installed, as in the Search Console section.)
- **Author archives:** *Single-author site →* turn author archives **OFF.** *Multi-author →* turn **ON** by setting **"Show author archives in search results" = ON** (optionally off for authors who haven't published yet).
- **Date archives:** **turn them OFF** (better); if you specifically need them for users, **assign noindex by choosing OFF.**
- **Taxonomies tab:** **Categories** — if you have a clear hierarchy and actively manage them, **show in search results** and configure SEO settings; otherwise turn **OFF** (users can still visit; Google just won't show them). **Tags** — same logic. **Post formats** — same.
- **Category URLs:** it's better to **remove the "category" slug** — e.g. `cocktailhub.in/seo` rather than `cocktailhub.in/category/seo` (better for SEO and user navigation).

---

## 🎬 Lecture 002 — 404 Error Pages in WordPress (Finding & Fixing "Page Not Found")

A **404** appears when a page can't be found — moved/deleted, or a mistyped URL.

### 🔎 Find 404s with Google Search Console
**Indexing → Pages → "All known pages" → under "Why pages aren't indexed" click "Not found (404)"** → view the list (or **export to Google Sheets**). These are URLs where either **Google once indexed the page but can't find it now,** or **followed a link to a non-existing page on your site.** **Periodically check and redirect them** to appropriate posts/pages (if you deleted a post/URL knowingly).

### 🔁 Redirect them with the **Redirection** plugin
1. **Plugins → Add New → "Redirection"** → install + activate.
2. **Tools → Redirection.** Shows redirects you've added and ones the plugin added automatically.
3. **Add New:** enter the **error URL (source)** and the **correct working URL (target)** → **Add redirect.** That's the standard procedure.
**Demo:** a 404 URL from GSC — `inspirearun.com/tag/motherboard-2/amp` — didn't work; removing `/amp` gave the working page → source = broken URL, target = working URL → test: it now redirects.

### 🤖 The plugin's own 404 log
Go to the plugin's **404s** page — it populates all errors it found. Most are **bots** (Googlebot, SEO-tool bots, even **hacking attempts** probing non-existing files). So you **needn't fix all** of them — **only validate and fix the URLs Google Search Console reports.** It's still a good starting point:
- Example: the tool flagged a **Stripe payment gateway URL** — clicking showed it actually works (**false positive**). Another showed "page not found" because Arun **changed his permalink structure** — he copied the correct post URL → **Add redirect** → target = the working post URL.
- **Bonus:** after installing the plugin, **if you change permalinks it automatically adds redirects** for affected pages, and **if you change a post's slug it adds a redirect for that too.**

### 🏷️ No suitable page to redirect to?
If you deliberately deleted many low-quality pages and have no appropriate target, **show a 404 page with an elegant search box and related/popular posts.**

**Benefits of a well-designed 404 page:** it turns frustration into an opportunity — **guide users back** with helpful info/links and **brand your site** for a positive impression.
**Tips:** **keep it simple · use humor sparingly · be helpful (links/resources) · include basics (logo, navigation) so users know they're still on your site · make it visually appealing.**

🔗 **Resource:** Redirection plugin — site options → https://redirection.me/support/site-options/

---

## 🎬 Lecture 003 — Canonical Tags in WordPress (Solving Duplicate Content with Yoast & Redirection)

**Canonical URL** = the URL considered **the official page.** When several pages cover the same topic with near-identical content, designate one as canonical. Also useful when your site is reachable at **both `www.example.com` and `example.com`** (search engines don't always know which to index → duplicate-content confusion).

### 🧪 The "RAM posts" example — why canonical instead of a 301
You have an old post about **RAM** (what it is, why we need it, how it works) **with valuable comments from registered customers.** You publish a new, more detailed, practical post (*"How much RAM will you need"*) that **includes all the old content,** with its own engagement. Options:
- **301-redirect to the new post** → old visitors **lose the valuable comments.**
- ✅ **Canonical tag:** in the **old post**, set the canonical to the **new post's URL** → search engines treat the old post as moved to the new post, **but previous visitors can still read the old post** (no redirect for humans); new searchers get the new post.

### 🛠️ Implement
- A **`rel="canonical"`** tag in the page's `<head>` pointing to the "true/master" URL. *(Per the lecture: it passes the same link juice as a 301, and is visible only to search engines — Google, Yahoo, Bing — not to real visitors.)*
- **With Yoast SEO:** copy the **master** post's URL → open the **old (duplicate)** post → **Edit** → scroll to the **Yoast SEO** box → expand the **Advanced** tab → paste the **full canonical URL** (including `https` and `www`/non-www as you prefer) in the **Canonical URL** field → **Update.** As a visitor you still see the old post; Google is pointed to the new one.

### 🌐 Fix the general canonical mistakes with the **Redirection** plugin
**Tools → Redirection → Site tab:**
- Enable **"Force a redirect from HTTP to HTTPS"** — only after you've **properly installed an SSL certificate and confirmed the HTTPS site loads without issues.**
- Set a **preferred domain** (Arun prefers non-www → redirect `www.yourdomain.com` to `yourdomain.com`); **choose one.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 004 — Common Canonical Tag Mistakes (And How to Avoid Them in WordPress)

### ❌ 5 common mistakes
1. **Canonicalizing paginated subpages to page 1.** If a **10,000-word post is split into ten pages,** don't set page 1 as the canonical for the rest — they aren't duplicates, just split. If you do want a canonical, point to **the complete single-page version** containing all 10,000 words (Google's recommendation).
2. **Using relative URLs.** Use the **absolute URL including `https`** — a link without `http/https` is treated as **relative.**
3. **Multiple canonical tags / pointing one post to multiple posts.** **Only one canonical tag and one target URL per post** — **if there are multiple canonical tags, Google neglects all of them.** (Often happens when **multiple plugins** handle canonical tags.)
4. **Canonicalizing a category (or landing) page to a featured article.** E.g. a dessert site with a **pastry** category: if you canonical that category to a daily featured article, **the category will never appear in Google search results.** Don't add canonicals to categories altogether.
5. **Putting the canonical tag in the `<body>`** — it must be inside the **`<head>`**.

### 📌 Other Google recommendations
- **Don't assign canonical tags unnecessarily** to every post you like — **assign them only to posts that have a considerable amount of valuable comments** (as the lecture frames it) — because **multiple canonical tags reduce the number of indexed pages.**
- **Double-check the canonical target exists** — otherwise it may show a **404.**
- **Make sure the target canonical page isn't `noindex`** — it must be indexable.

---

## ✅ Section 24 Recap

- **Duplicate content from archives:** `noindex` unmanaged **tag, author, date, category** archives (Yoast → Search Appearance → Archives/Taxonomies); **don't** noindex where categories/tags are landing pages (e-commerce, managed hierarchies). Make indexed ones unique: hierarchy, single singular/plural tag, **100–300 words** + FAQs; remove `/category/` from URLs.
- **404s:** find in GSC (Indexing → Pages → Not found 404); fix with **Redirection plugin** (source → target, 301); only fix GSC-reported URLs (the plugin's own log is mostly bots, with false positives); plugin auto-redirects after permalink/slug changes; for deleted low-value pages show a **helpful custom 404** (search box, popular posts, logo/nav, simple, light humor).
- **Canonicals in WordPress:** use when old content with valuable comments should stay visible but a newer post should rank (RAM example); Yoast → Advanced → Canonical URL; Redirection → Site tab → force HTTP→HTTPS + preferred domain (www vs non-www).
- **Canonical mistakes:** paginated subpages, relative URLs, multiple tags, category→featured article, tag in `<body>`; plus: don't overuse, ensure target exists and is indexable.

**🔗 Section resources:** Section Notes → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/notes-wordpress-technical-seo · Resource library → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/ · Redirection plugin options → https://redirection.me/support/site-options/


> 🧐 **How to read the 🔄 blocks:** everything above this line is the course *as taught* (kept unchanged). The block below is an independent *objector* review (researched 2026-10-24) saying where the old way is outdated, wrong or beatable, and what a senior practitioner would do instead. Items marked *unverified* rest on secondary sources. A second pass re-checked the highest-impact claims against primary sources; its corrections are shown in the 🔬 box at the top of each affected block, and the full evidence is in the final appendix.

## 🔄 Objector's Review — Better / Updated Way (Section 24)

**1. Lecture ref:** 001 (noindex tag/author/date/category archives; "increase indexed unique-content ratio"; Yoast Search Appearance toggles)
**Old way:** Noindex unmanaged archives in Yoast; remove /category/.
**Verdict:** ⚠️
**Better way:** Principle sound (thin/duplicate archives dilute crawl & quality; keep managed category/tag pages indexable with unique copy). Corrections: (1) Google's doc: a noindex page is crawled at least once to see the directive; never combine noindex with a robots.txt Disallow (the directive becomes unreadable); noindex in robots.txt is unsupported. (2) "Unique-content ratio" is an SEO folk metric — no Google source (unverified). (3) Google advises against using noindex as a canonicalization tool within a site. (4) For single-author sites, WordPress/Yoast now disable author archives by default or via toggle, and date archives are typically off. (5) Use `noindex, follow` and keep archives out of XML sitemaps; also consider noindexing internal search (`/?s=`) and tag pages with <3 posts. (6) Yoast's UI changed (Settings > Content types / Categories & tags / Archives) vs the course's "Search Appearance" screens, and Rank Math/AIOSEO/SEOPress offer equivalent per-taxonomy toggles. (7) Category base removal: Yoast's own help says removing the prefix "can cause unexpected URL behavior and is not recommended in most cases"; Rank Math calls it "Strip Category Base" and warns about slug collisions with posts/pages/products. Teach: if removing, add 301s from /category/x/ to /x/, verify no slug collisions, and test with a crawl — not a blind recommendation. The `/seo` vs `/category/seo` SEO benefit is unproven (unverified). (8) Plugin choice: Rank Math (richest free tier incl. redirects/404 monitor/schema), Yoast (conservative, best guidance), AIOSEO (multi-site pricing), SEOPress (flat unlimited-site pricing) — based on 2026 comparison blogs; pricing conflicts across sources, so verify. None needed for AIO; do not buy "AI-optimisation" add-ons for llms.txt on the strength of marketing (Google ignores it for Search).
**New tools/skills:** URL Inspection, Screaming Frog, claude-seo:seo-technical, plugin "robots/indexing" bulk editors; claude-seo:seo-sitemap.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/block-indexing (2026-10-24); https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24); https://yoast.com/help/how-to-remove-the-categories-prefix/ (2026-10-24); https://rankmath.com/kb/remove-wordpress-category-base/ ; https://belovdigital.agency/the-best-wordpress-seo-plugins-for-2026/ and https://oddjar.com/wordpress-seo-plugins-2026-comparison/ (secondary).

**2. Lecture ref:** 002 (404s: GSC "Not found (404)", Redirection plugin, plugin 404 log, custom 404 page)
**Old way:** Redirect GSC 404s with Redirection; log mostly bots.
**Verdict:** ⚠️
**Better way:** Mostly right (fix only meaningful 404s, check intent). Updates: (1) a 404 is NOT a problem by itself; Google says 404 for removed pages is fine — redirect only if there is a closely equivalent page or the URL has backlinks/traffic; mass-redirecting dead URLs to the homepage is treated as a soft 404. For truly gone content return 404 or 410. (2) Prioritise 404s with backlinks (Ahrefs/Semrush/GSC Links) and internal links; fix the internal link source, don't redirect around it. (3) Use server-level rules (Nginx/Apache/Cloudflare bulk redirects) for large migrations; Redirection plugin logging can bloat the DB — cap log retention and disable 404 logging when idle. (4) Redirection's automatic slug/permalink redirects are real, but verify with a crawl. (5) Soft-404 and "Crawled – currently not indexed" are separate GSC states to check. (6) Custom 404 must still return HTTP 404 (not 200).
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog list-mode redirect testing, Cloudflare Bulk Redirects, httpstatus checks, claude-seo:seo-drift (post-deploy diffs).
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/http-network-errors (not fetched = unverified wording); Redirection plugin docs https://redirection.me/support/site-options/ (from course, not verified).

**3. Lecture ref:** 003 (Canonical: "passes the same link juice as a 301", visible only to search engines; RAM example: canonical old post to new)
**Old way:** Use rel=canonical when 301 would lose comments; manual per-post canonical in Yoast.
**Verdict:** ⚠️ / ❌ (RAM example)
**Better way:** Google's doc: canonical is a "strong signal," not a directive; redirects are listed as the strongest/first method; both are signals. Canonical is for near-duplicate/duplicate pages. The RAM example canonicals two pages that are not duplicates (old post has different, shorter content) and Google may ignore the hint; also canonical-ed old posts drop out of search, comments and all. Better senior approach: consolidate (merge the old post into the new one, import the useful comments/Q&A into a new section, then 301), or keep both if they serve different intents. Keep self-referencing absolute canonicals by default (the SEO plugin does it); only override for true duplicates (syndication, parameters, print versions). HTTP->HTTPS and www/non-www: configure at server/CDN/host (HSTS, single 301 hop), not via the Redirection plugin where possible, and set WordPress Address/Site Address correctly. "Same link juice as a 301" is not a Google-published claim (unverified).
**New tools/skills:** URL Inspection (user vs Google-selected canonical), Screaming Frog canonical report, claude-seo:seo-technical.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24).

**4. Lecture ref:** 004 (Canonical mistakes: paginated subpages, relative URLs, multiple tags, category->featured, body placement; "only post with comments")
**Old way:** List of five mistakes; assign canonical only to posts with valuable comments.
**Verdict:** ⚠️
**Better way:** Valid: one canonical, in `<head>`, target must be indexable/200, not canonical to a noindex or 404 page. Corrections: (1) Relative URLs are allowed by spec but absolute is safer — fine. (2) "Assign canonicals only to posts with comments / canonicals reduce indexed pages" is not a Google recommendation (unverified); self-canonicals on all indexable pages are best practice. (3) Pagination: each paginated URL self-canonicals; Google no longer uses rel=prev/next; paginated comment pages and "view all" should be handled by internal linking, not canonicalising page 2 to page 1. (4) Multi-plugin canonical conflicts are real: run one SEO plugin only; check theme/page builder/Jetpack/WooCommerce add-ons; test with View-source + URL Inspection. (5) JavaScript-injected canonicals can conflict with the HTML canonical — keep it server-rendered. (6) Add canonical in HTTP header for PDFs.
**New tools/skills:** Screaming Frog, Sitebulb, Rich Results/URL Inspection live test, claude-seo:seo-audit.
**Sources:** https://developers.google.com/search/docs/crawling-indexing/consolidate-duplicate-urls (2026-10-24); pagination change (Google dropped rel=prev/next in 2019) — official Search Central tweet/blog, not re-fetched = unverified wording.

**5. Lecture ref:** WordPress 6.x changes (not covered)
**Old way:** n/a
**Verdict:** ❌ (omission)
**Better way:** Not researched in depth this session = unverified. Known items to check before teaching: block themes/Site Editor, native lazy-load and `fetchpriority` for the LCP image, WebP/AVIF upload support, Performance Lab plugin, speculative-loading (prerender) in core (6.8, verify). Treat as a checklist, not source-backed claims.
**New tools/skills:** WordPress Performance Lab plugin, WP-CLI for bulk redirect/permalink audits.
**Sources:** none fetched — unverified.

# SECTION 25 — SEO Career Path: From Beginner to Highly-Paid Professional

**Lectures:** 001 Why SEO careers are booming · 002 What employers really want · 003 Irreplaceable skills AI can't replicate · 004 Strategic thinking · 005 The data-skill gap (30% higher salaries) · 006 Inside the SEO job market · 007 Ace your SEO interview · 008 Launch your freelance business · 009 Pricing your SEO services · 010 Scale your freelance business · 011 Overcome the 3 biggest job-seeker challenges · 012 Problems faced by working SEOs · 013 Top freelance SEO challenges · 014 Navigating industry challenges · 015 The SEO mindset: 3 mental shifts · 016 The real cost of starting · 017 The time investment · 018 Your next action step.
*Each lecture has attached "Quick Guide" PDFs (numbered 1–12 plus a burnout worksheet); their contents are included below under 📎 where they belong.*

---

## 🎬 Lecture 001 — Why SEO Careers Are Booming (And Where You Fit In)

- **The industry is booming:** *organic search drives over **53%** of all website traffic;* businesses that aren't optimizing miss **half their potential customers.** The SEO industry is **projected to grow ~15% annually through 2030.**
- **Clear progression path:** **entry level → specialization → management → leadership.** As you advance you'll likely branch into one of **three specialized paths** based on your strengths:
  1. **SEO Analyst** — the **data-focused** path
  2. **Content SEO Specialist**
  3. **Technical SEO Specialist**
  Salaries rise significantly as you build specialized expertise. *(The lecture shows typical salary ranges by designation on screen; pause to read — the PDF below expands on it.)*

### 💰 Four main ways to earn a substantial income in SEO
| Path | Benefit |
|---|---|
| **SEO agency employment** | Stable income + additional benefits |
| **In-house SEO for businesses** | Competitive salaries with potential equity down the line |
| **Freelancing** | Unlimited earning potential + flexibility |
| **SEO consulting** | Premium rates for specialized expertise |
Many successful professionals **combine these: start with stable employment while building skills, then move to higher-paying freelance work or consulting.**

### 🎯 Your first strategic move
Apply what you've learned: **identify two SEO roles aligned with your career goals,** then **research companies hiring for them.** Ask: *Which roles match my strengths and interests? What timeline is realistic? Which specializations intrigue me most?* — a simple exercise that gives clarity.

### 📎 Quick Guide 1 — *SEO Salary Roadmap: What Can You Earn?*
**"SEO pays well, but it depends on key things":**
- **Your experience (biggest factor):** **Entry-level (0–2 yrs):** focus on learning · **Mid-level (2–5 yrs):** handling projects, showing results · **Senior (5+ yrs):** deep expertise, strategy, maybe leading others.
- **Location:** big cities (**San Francisco, New York, London**) usually pay more (living costs + demand); **remote jobs vary.**
- **Company type:** **Agencies** might start lower but give broad experience · **In-house (client-side):** big tech/e-commerce often pay top dollar; smaller businesses may offer less cash but other perks · **Startups:** a mix — maybe lower salary but potential stock options.
- **Your skills:** **Technical SEO and data analysis** often command higher salaries (complex, in demand); **niche expertise** (e.g. **healthcare, finance**) helps.
- **Demonstrated impact:** can you show how your work produced more leads/sales/traffic? That's **negotiation power.**
- **Negotiation:** know your worth and ask for it.
- **Freelancing:** can eventually earn more than a salary, but **income is less predictable at first;** you need **business skills (finding clients, managing projects);** factor in **taxes and benefits (health insurance, time off)** that salaries include.
- **Key takeaways for beginners:** ① **Skills first** — good pay follows good work. ② **Research** typical ranges on **Glassdoor or LinkedIn Salary** for your location and target role. ③ **Track your wins** — even small project successes show value. ④ **Be patient.**
- **Action:** visit 1–2 salary websites and look up **"SEO Specialist"** in your city to get a realistic starting point.

---

## 🎬 Lecture 002 — What SEO Employers Really Want (Beyond What Job Listings Say)

Arun spent **months researching hiring trends and talking with industry leaders.**

> **AI is reshaping everything.** Google's **AI Overviews (previously Search Generative Experience)** are becoming central to results, potentially **decreasing organic CTR, especially for informational keywords.** But *"this isn't the SEO apocalypse — just an evolution, similar to when mobile search and featured snippets emerged."*

**Employers still expect strong fundamentals** — consistently looking for:
1. **Keyword research** with a focus on **user intent, not just volume or traffic.**
2. **On-page optimization** tailored to **users, search engines, *and* AI chatbots (ChatGPT, Perplexity…).**
3. **Technical SEO understanding**, especially **site architecture and performance.**
4. **Proficiency with tools:** **Google Analytics, Search Console, and premium tools like SEMrush.**

**What's changed:** they don't want people who are *just good at this* — they want people who can **translate technical skills into business impact:** identify **why traffic fluctuates** and **communicate solutions effectively.**

---

## 🎬 Lecture 003 — The Irreplaceable SEO Skills AI Can't Replicate

To be truly invaluable, focus on skills AI can't easily replicate:
1. **Advanced technical SEO** (everything from Section 14): **site architecture, crawl budget management, structured data implementation.** Practice and master it.
2. **Working knowledge of HTML, CSS and JavaScript basics** — you needn't write or understand every line, but you should be able to **add or change a couple of lines of code here and there, using AI when necessary.**
3. Two more that make you irreplaceable: **strategic thinking** and **data interpretation** (next two lectures).

---

## 🎬 Lecture 004 — Strategic Thinking: The Skill That Doubles Your SEO Value

**What it is:** businesses with long-term vision **don't care about traffic, keywords or even rankings — they care about **revenue and ROI (return on investment).** That's why **the first four hours of this course (the first five sections, incl. keyword research and topical authority) were designed to develop your strategic thinking.** **Re-watch them now** — your improved understanding will sharpen your ability to **turn business objectives into appropriate SEO goals.**

**Common student question:** *how do you balance technical expertise with strategic thinking?*
1. **Start with one technical specialty that interests you** — structured data, Core Web Vitals, or crawl-budget optimization.
2. **For every technical skill you learn, practice explaining its business impact in simple terms.**
3. **Develop a framework to prioritize technical fixes by potential ROI.** *Example:* **fixing a crawl error blocking 50% of your product pages** should come before **optimizing all image alt text** — the first is **high impact with potentially low effort;** the second is **medium impact with potentially high effort.**
4. **Create case studies with before/after metrics** for your technical implementations.

### 🧩 The explanation template
Most people can fix technical issues but struggle to explain why. Arun's template:
> **"By fixing *this technical issue* we can improve *this user-experience factor,* which typically increases *this business metric* by *this percentage range.*"**
Using it "can land you a good SEO position even at a company that might previously have put you on a waiting list."

---

## 🎬 Lecture 005 — The Data Skill Gap That Leads to 30% Higher SEO Salaries

**Data interpretation** = "the differentiator between average and exceptional SEO professionals." Employers expect you to **turn complex data from Google Analytics and Search Console into actionable insights to improve revenue and ROI — not just traffic.** Arun has seen candidates with strong data-interpretation skills receive **20–30% higher salary offers.** It's not about knowing the tools — it's **applying them strategically to solve business problems** (which needs strategic thinking first).

**How to build advanced data skills:** Google's **free certification program via Skillshop** and the **Search Console training series on the Google Search Central YouTube channel** (links attached).

> 📈 *Example:* on an **e-commerce site**, product pages with **one specific type of structured data implementation had 34% higher conversion rates** — **not obvious in standard Google Analytics reports; it required custom segments and explorations.** Finding such insights is what makes you irreplaceable.

### 📎 Quick Guide 2 — *Core Data Skills Training Links*
- **Prioritize completing both free official trainings;** they give foundational knowledge directly from Google. **Action:** visit the Skillshop link and **bookmark the GA4 courses;** visit the YouTube link and **start the first Search Console video.**
- **① GA4 Certification & Training — Google Skillshop** (https://skillshop.withgoogle.com/ → Google Analytics section): learn **basic setup & navigation, event-based tracking, custom reports & analysis, user behavior flows, conversion tracking;** benefit: earn the **official Google Analytics Certification** (great for resume & LinkedIn). *Specific course links inside Skillshop change — always start from the main link.*
- **② Google Search Console Training Series — Google Search Central YouTube channel:** learn **Performance reports (queries, clicks, impressions…), submitting sitemaps, the URL Inspection tool, fixing indexing errors (Coverage report), monitoring Core Web Vitals & Mobile Usability, Manual Actions & Security Issues, analyzing links;** benefit: understand how Google sees a site and diagnose technical issues directly. *If the playlist link breaks, search "Google Search Console Training" on the Google Search Central channel.*

🔗 **Resource:** *Learning & Certification: Official Google Training (Analytics, Search Central)* → https://aruntastic.com/resources/library/seo/seo-career-path
